The Alans made peace with him and gave him the jizya and land tax. He was the first to return by way of the Gate of the Alans.”

In it, Bishr b. Ṣafwān, while he was in charge of Ifriqiya, sent Muḥammad b. Abī Bakr, the client of Banū Jumāḥ, on a campaign, and he captured Corsica and Sardinia.

In it, Saʿīd b. ʿAbd al-Malik campaigned in the land of the Romans, and Hishām b. ʿAbd al-Malik led the pilgrimage. In the year 100, Ṭāwūs b. Kaysān died in Mecca, and Hishām b. ʿAbd al-Malik, before he went to Minā. In it, Muslim b. Jundub al-Hudhalī died. In it, Muʿāwiya b. Hishām led the summer campaign. In it, Khālid b. ʿAbd Allāh al-Qasrī was appointed over Iraq.

The Year 107

In it, Hishām b. ʿAbd al-Malik dismissed al-Jarrāḥ b. ʿAbd Allāh al-Ḥakamī from Armenia and Azerbaijan and appointed Maslama b. ʿAbd al-Malik over them. Maslama dispatched al-Ḥārith b. ʿAmr al-Ṭāʾī. Abū Khālid said: Abū Barāʾ said: “Al-Ḥārith campaigned and captured a district called Khashdān, in the lands of al-Kurr.”

Abū Barāʾ said: “Maslama also campaigned that year. He crossed the frontier from Malatya and encamped against Qaysāriyya, which he captured by force on the fourth day of Ramaḍān of the year 107.”

Ibn al-Kalbī said: “In it, Muʿāwiya b. Hishām campaigned in the land of the Romans. His army reached [there], and he sent al-Waḍḍāḥ, the commander of al-Waḍḍāḥiyya, who burned the villages and crops and cut down the trees.”

Ibn al-Kalbī said: “In that year, a severe plague broke out in Syria, until it affected the beasts of burden and cattle.”

In it, Asad b. ʿAbd Allāh campaigned in Gharjistān and sent Ibn Sālim al-Azdī in command of the cavalry. A force of the enemy met him, and a number of Muslims were killed. Famine struck the people that year, and they returned exhausted. In it, Muʿāwiya b. Hishām campaigned in the land of the Romans; he reached Arwaliya. Ibrāhīm b. Hishām b. Ismāʿīl al-Makhzūmī led the pilgrimage.

In the year 107, Sālim b. ʿAbd Allāh b. ʿUmar died at the beginning of the year; al-Qāsim b. Muḥammad b. Abī Bakr died at the end of the year; and ʿAṭāʾ b. Yazīd al-Laythī died. In it, al-Muʿtamir b. Sulaymān and Sufyān b. ʿUyayna were born. At the beginning of Hishām’s caliphate, Yazīd and Muḥammad, the two sons of Ṭalḥa b. Rukāna, died, as did Muslim b. Jundub.

The Year 108

In it, Asad b. ʿAbd Allāh campaigned in Ghūr. They met him in a great force and fought fiercely; then God routed the enemy.

In it, Maslama b. ʿAbd al-Malik led the right-hand summer campaign, and ʿĀṣim b. ʿAbd Allāh b. Yazīd al-Hilālī led the left-hand summer campaign. In it, the son of the Khāqān advanced into Azerbaijan.

Abū Khālid said: Abū Barāʾ said: “Mā Rātik b. Khāqān advanced into Azerbaijan in the year 108. He besieged the city of Warthān and bombarded it with mangonels. The report reached al-Ḥārith b. ʿAmr, who set out and cut off the river above Warthān. It reached Ibn Khāqān, who came to al-Ḥārith. They met, and God routed Ibn Khāqān and his companions. He killed a great number of them, and al-Ḥārith b. ʿAmr was killed ﵀.”

In it, Bishr b. Ṣafwān sent out Qutham b. ʿAwāna al-Kalbī from Ifriqiya on a campaign; he gained booty and returned safely. Ibrāhīm b. Hishām b. Ismāʿīl led the pilgrimage.

In it, ʿAbbād al-Ḥarūrī revolted in al-Rayy, and Yūsuf b. ʿUmar killed him. In it, Muʿāwiya b. Hishām campaigned in the land of the Romans and sent al-Baṭṭāl to Khanjara, which he captured. In this year, which was the year 108, Abū al-ʿAlāʾ Yazīd b. ʿAbd Allāh b. al-Shikhkhīr al-Ḥarashī died.

Bakr b. ʿAbd Allāh al-Muzanī, Abū al-Malīḥ al-Hudhalī, Abū Naḍra al-ʿAbdī, Abū Ḥarb b. Abī al-Aswad al-Duʾalī, and Khālid b. Maʿdān al-Shāmī. After the hundred, Abū Shaykh al-Hunāʾī died, as did ʿAbd Allāh b. Shaqīq al-ʿUqaylī, Saʿīd b. Abī al-Ḥasan, Abū al-Mutawakkil al-Nājī, and Abū al-Ṣiddīq al-Nājī.

The Year 109

In it, Bishr b. Ṣafwān sent Ḥassān b. Muḥammad b. Abī Bakr, the client of Banū Jumāḥ, from Ifriqiya to Sardinia. He gained booty and returned safely. In it, Bishr b. Ṣafwān died in Ifriqiya and appointed Nuʿās b. Qurṭ al-Kalbī as his successor. In it, Maslama b. ʿAbd al-Malik campaigned and sent armies into Azerbaijan, where they wintered. In it, Muʿāwiya b. Hishām campaigned in the land of the Romans and captured a fortress called al-Ghaṭṭāsīn. Ibrāhīm b. Hishām b. Ismāʿīl led the pilgrimage. In this year, which was the year 109, Abū Najīḥ—Abū ʿAbd Allāh b. Abī Najīḥ—died, as did Dafīf, the client of Ibn ʿAbbās, and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān b. Saʿīd b. Yarbuʿ.

The Year 110

The Campaign of Mud: In it, Maslama b. ʿAbd al-Malik campaigned in the lands of the Khazars. This is the campaign called the Campaign of Mud. Abū Khālid, from Abū Barāʾ al-Numayrī, said: “Maslama set out toward Tilmīs and encountered...”

...the tyrant of the Khazars, with a great force, near the gate. They fought for many days; then God routed them. That was on Thursday, seven nights having passed in Jumādā al-Ākhira of the year 110. Abū Barāʾ said: ʿAbd Allāh b. Usayd al-Kilābī related to me that Maslama returned from the Gate of the Alans. The Khazars encountered him and skirmished with him until night came between them, and Maslama returned safely.

Ibn al-Kalbī said: “Maslama’s fighting against them lasted about a month, in heavy rain; then God routed them.” In it, Muʿāwiya b. Hishām campaigned in the land of the Romans and captured two of their fortresses: Ṣamla and al-Buwa. Abū Khālid said: “In it, ʿUbayda b. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Dhakwānī, of Banū Sulaym, arrived from Ifriqiya. He sent ʿUthmān b. Abī ʿUbayda on a campaign with seven hundred men. He set out for the city of Syracuse in Sicily. They met him, their commander was captured, and God routed them. Ibrāhīm b. Hishām b. Ismāʿīl al-Makhzūmī led the pilgrimage. In the year 110, al-Ḥasan b. Abī al-Ḥasan died in Rajab, and al-Naḍr b. ʿAmr al-Muqriʾ, of Ḥimyar and from among the people of Syria, led the funeral prayer over him.

In it, Muḥammad b. Sīrīn died in Shawwāl, and al-Naḍr b. ʿAmr led the funeral prayer over him. In it, al-Farazdaq died, and Jarīr died some months after him. Ibn al-Kalbī said: “In it, ʿAbd al-Malik b. Yasār, the brother of ʿAṭāʾ and Sulaymān, the two sons of Yasār, died.” In it, Wahb b. Munabbih and Ibrāhīm b. Muḥammad b. Ṭalḥa b. ʿUbayd Allāh died.

The Year 111

In it, Ashras ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Sulamī sent to the rulers of Ṭukhāristān. They came to him, and there was no campaign for them. In it, Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik removed his brother Maslama from Armenia and Azerbaijan and appointed al-Jarrāḥ ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Ḥakamī to the governorship for a second time.

Abū Khālid said: Abū al-Khaṭṭāb said: Al-Jarrāḥ assumed the governorship for a second time in the year 111. He came to Tiflis and raided a Khazar city called al-Bayḍāʾ, capturing it, then withdrew. The Khazars gathered a great host with the son of the Khāqān. They entered Armenia, and the son of the Khāqān marched and besieged the people of Ardabīl. Ibn al-Kalbī said: In it, Muʿāwiya ibn Hishām campaigned with the left wing of the summer expedition and encountered no resistance. In it, Saʿīd ibn Hishām also campaigned in the summer expedition from the direction of al-Jazīra, reaching Qaysāriyya.

Abū Khālid said: In it, ʿUbayda ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān sent al-Mustanīr ibn al-Ḥārith from Ifrīqiya in 180 vessels. He landed and besieged them. When winter set in, he returned with a favorable wind until he was far out at sea; then a violent wind arose and sank their vessels. Only seventeen vessels among them survived.

Ibrāhīm ibn Hishām led the pilgrimage. In it, Tamīm ibn Aws died; he had been a judge. In it, ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Rāfiʿ ibn Khudayj died.

The Year 112

In it, Ashras ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Sulamī campaigned in Farghāna. The enemy host met him, and the Turks surrounded him. This reached Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, who appointed al-Junayd ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Murrī as governor of Marra Ghatafān. In it, al-Jarrāḥ marched from Bardhaʿa in the year 112. He came to Azerbaijan and encamped in the plain of Mount Sabalān, where there was a river. He built a bridge over it, which to this day is called al-Jarrāḥ’s Bridge.

Abū Khālid said: Abū Barāʾ said: In the year 112, al-Jarrāḥ marched against the son of the Khāqān, who was besieging the people of Ardabīl. They fought fiercely, and al-Jarrāḥ ﵀—was killed with eight nights remaining in Ramaḍān of the year 112. The Khazars gained control of Azerbaijan, and their cavalry ranged about until they came near al-Mawṣil. They set up mangonels against Ardabīl, while its people fought them. When the siege grew long for them, they surrendered the city, and the Khazars entered it, killing the fighting men and taking the children captive.

Abū Khālid said: Abū al-Khaṭṭāb said: A man from Banū Sulaym related to me: Al-Jarrāḥ was killed at Arshaq. Abū al-Khaṭṭāb said: When al-Jarrāḥ was killed, Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik sent Saʿīd ibn ʿAmr al-Ḥarashī and sent with him the horsemen of the Arabs by the postal route. Saʿīd ibn ʿAmr proceeded until he came to Bardhaʿa. ʿAlqama al-Thaqafī, reporting from a shaykh from the people of Ḥimṣ, said: Thubayt al-Bahrānī related to me: Al-Ḥarashī came to us at Bardhaʿa on the postal mounts. We went with them to al-Baylaqān, and they proceeded toward Azerbaijan. A Khazar army approached, with many wagons carrying Muslim captives and the spoils taken from the people of Ardabīl.

Thubayt said: Al-Ḥarashī sent me ahead as a scout. I came upon the army while they were asleep, then returned and informed him. He urged on his companions and marched against them, rescuing the wagons and their contents.

Thubayt said: He sent me to the city of al-Baylaqān and wrote to Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik informing him of the victory. Then he was also informed of many wagons in the direction of Warthān, carrying captives and booty. He attacked them by night, killed those of the enemy who were in them, brought the wagons into the city of Warthān, and wrote to Hishām informing him of the victory. He then set out and encountered the Khazar ruler. God routed them, and the ruler fled. Al-Ḥarashī recovered the Muslim captives and their booty that he had with him.

Abū Khālid said: Abū Barāʾ said: ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-ʿĀmirī related to me that Saʿīd ibn ʿAmr al-Ḥarashī encountered the son of the Khāqān and attacked them by night. He inflicted a great slaughter on them; the Khazar ruler fled, and he wrote to Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik informing him of the victory. Ibn al-Kalbī said: Al-Jarrāḥ and those with him were martyred in the plain of Ardabīl. He had appointed his brother al-Ḥajjāj ibn ʿAbd Allāh as his deputy. Al-Ḥarashī came upon them, God routed them, and he recovered what was in their hands.

Ibn al-Kalbī said: Maslama ibn ʿAbd al-Malik set out in Shawwāl of the year 112 to pursue the Turks, amid severe rain and snow, until he crossed the gate. He left al-Ṭāʾī to build the gate and fortify it, and dispatched a force for that purpose. Then he sent out the armies, which captured cities and fortresses. The enemies of God burned themselves with fire inside their cities. In the year 112, Abū Khālid said: In it, ʿUbayda ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān sent Thābit ibn Khaytham, one of the people of al-Urdunn, from Ifrīqiya to Sicily. He took captives and booty and returned safely. Ibn al-Kalbī said: In it, Muʿāwiya ibn Hishām campaigned in the summer expedition and captured Ḥarshana in the vicinity of Malatya. Ibrāhīm ibn Ismāʿīl al-Makhzūmī led the pilgrimage. In this year, Rajāʾ ibn Ḥaywa and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Abī Saʿīd al-Khudrī died.

The Year 113

In it, al-Junayd ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān set out from Marra Ghatafān on campaign, intending for Ṭukhāristān. The Turks surged against Samarkand, and al-Junayd marched until he was four [units] from Samarkand. The Khāqān met him, and they fought fiercely until evening, when they separated. Al-Junayd wrote to Sūra ibn Abjar of Banū Abān ibn Dārim, his governor over Samarkand, ordering him to come to him. The Turks met Sūra before he reached al-Junayd, killed Sūra ibn Abjar and the bulk of his army, and Mujāhid ibn Balʿāʾ al-ʿAnbarī was killed with him. Then al-Junayd met them, and God routed them. Al-Junayd proceeded and entered Samarkand.

In the year 113, Hishām appointed his brother Maslama over Armenia and Azerbaijan and dismissed Saʿīd ibn ʿAmr al-Ḥarashī. Maslama appointed ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama, then marched, seized Saʿīd ibn ʿAmr, shackled him, and imprisoned him. Hishām sent orders, and he was released from prison.

Abū Khālid said: Abū Barāʾ said: Maslama arrived and asked the people of Ḥīzān for a treaty, but they refused. He fought them, and they asked him for safe-conduct. He swore that he would kill neither a man among them nor a dog. They came down, and he killed them all except one man and one dog. He took the fortress and marched to the land of Sūzān. The king asked him for a treaty, and he made peace with him; he also made peace with the people of Masqaṭ and the people of al-Karr. Maslama met the Khāqān, and they fought fiercely. Maslama then withdrew and came to Ghazāla. The Khazar armies surrounded him, and they fought fiercely. Then God routed the Khazars, and the Khāqān fled.

Abū Khālid said: Abū al-Khaṭṭāb said: When Maslama was advancing, the Khazars marched against him. Maslama did not notice them until they appeared before him, and he fought them. Night came between them. The Muslims spent the night keeping vigil, while the Khazars withdrew. Maslama returned and appointed Marwān ibn Muḥammad as his deputy. All of that took place in the year 113.

Abū Khālid said: In the year 113, ʿUbayda ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān sent ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Qaṭan from Ifrīqiya. He came to Sicily, took booty, and returned safely.

In it, he also sent Abū ʿImrān al-Hudhalī, who took booty and returned safely. Sulaymān ibn Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik led the pilgrimage. In the year 113, Makḥūl al-Shāmī died in Syria; Ṭalḥa ibn Muṣarrif al-Ayāmī died in Kūfa; Yūsuf ibn Māhak died in Mecca; ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUbayd ibn ʿUmayr died in Mecca; and Ḥarām ibn Saʿd ibn Muḥayyiṣa died. One who heard al-Walīd ibn Muslim, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Yazīd ibn Jābir, related to us: He said: Makḥūl died in the year 113.

The Year 114

In it, Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik removed Maslama ibn ʿAbd al-Malik from Armenia, Azerbaijan, and al-Jazīra, and appointed Marwān ibn Muḥammad ibn Marwān over them, effective from the beginning of Muḥarram of the year 114.

Abū Khālid said: Abū al-Barāʾ said: In the year 114, Marwān marched until he crossed the Nahr al-Ram. He killed, took captives, and raided the Slavs.

Abū Khālid said: In it, ʿUbayda ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān also sent ʿAbd Allāh ibn Qaṭan from Ifrīqiya to Sicily. He took booty and returned safely. He also sent ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ziyād al-Anṣārī to Sardinia. He took booty and returned safely.

In it, al-Junayd ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān campaigned in al-Ṣaghāniyān, encountered no resistance, and withdrew.

Ibn al-Kalbī said: In it, Muʿāwiya ibn Hishām campaigned in the land of the Romans. ʿAbd Allāh al-Baṭṭāl and Constantine met in a force, and God routed the enemy, and Constantine was captured.

In it, Sulaymān ibn Hishām campaigned in the land of the Romans from the direction of al-Jazīra, reaching Qaysāriyya. Khālid ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Ḥakam ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ led the pilgrimage. In the year 114, al-Ḥakam ibn ʿUtayba died.

The Year 115

In it, al-Ḥārith ibn Surayj rose up and gained control of al-Jūzjān and Marw. Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik sent ʿĀṣim ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yazīd al-Hilālī, who met al-Ḥārith ibn Surayj. They fought fiercely, then made peace on the terms that al-Ḥārith ibn Surayj would stay in Balkh and send an envoy to Hishām, and that ʿĀṣim would send an envoy. Khālid sent his brother Asad ibn ʿAbd Allāh as governor over Khurāsān and dismissed ʿĀṣim. Al-Ḥārith crossed the Balkh River, and Asad marched. They met, and al-Ḥārith was routed and fled to the Turks. Asad ibn ʿAbd Allāh seized some of his companions, killed some of them, and cut off the hands and feet of others.

In it, Muʿāwiya ibn Hishām campaigned during the month of Ramaḍān, reaching Aflājūniya.

Abū Khālid said: In the year 115, ʿUbayda ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān sent Bakr ibn Suwayd from Ifrīqiya. He came to Sicily and Darbāna, where the Romans encountered him and hurled fire at his vessels. Muḥammad ibn Hishām ibn Ismāʿīl led the pilgrimage. In the year 115, ʿAṭāʾ ibn Abī Rabāḥ died; it is also said that he died in the year 116.

The Year 116

In it, Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik wrote to ʿUbayda ibn al-Ḥabḥāb, the client of Banū Salūl and his governor over Egypt, and appointed him over Ifrīqiya. He entered it in the year 116. ʿAbd al-Aʿlā ibn Ḥudayj, the client of Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr, who was a Ṣufrī, rose up at Tangier. ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-ʿAbsī, who was governor for Ibn al-Ḥabḥāb, went out against him. ʿAmr was killed, and his companions were routed.

In it, Ibn al-Ḥabḥāb sent ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥabīb ibn Abī ʿUbayda ibn ʿUqba ibn Nāfiʿ to the Sūs and the land of the Blacks. He prevailed and obtained much gold.

In it, Ibn al-Ḥabḥāb also sent ʿUthmān ibn Abī ʿUbayda, who raided a district of Sicily and returned. Roman vessels encountered him at sea, and God routed them. They inflicted losses among the Muslims and captured ʿUthmān’s two sons, ʿAmr and Sulaymān Abū al-Rabīʿ, as well as ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ziyād ibn Anʿam and his brother al-Mughīra ibn Ziyād. They remained in Roman hands until ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥabīb became governor. He ransomed his two cousins, some Muslim prisoners, and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ziyād. That was in the year 121.

Al-Walīd ibn Yazīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik led the pilgrimage. It is also said that ʿĪsā ibn Miqsam, the client of al-Walīd, did so by al-Walīd’s command. In it, Maymūn ibn Mihrān died in al-Jazīra.

The Year 117

In it, the Turks advanced into Khurāsān, with al-Ḥārith ibn Surayj among them. The Khāqān, with al-Ḥārith, reached al-Jūzjān, and the Turks raided until they came to Marw al-Rūdh.

Then one who had heard Abū al-Dhayyāl related to me. He said: Asad ibn ʿAbd Allāh marched, met them, and God routed them; the Muslims killed them in a terrible slaughter.

Abū Khālid said, from Abū Barāʾ, who said: In it, Marwān ibn Muḥammad, governor over Armenia and Azerbaijan, sent two expeditions to Mount al-Qabq. One of the two expeditions captured three fortresses belonging to al-Lān. The other expedition came upon Tūmān Shāh, and Tūmān Shāh submitted to Marwān ibn Muḥammad’s terms. Marwān sent him to Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, but Hishām returned him to Marwān. Marwān restored him to his kingdom. Abū Khālid said: In it, ʿUbayda ibn al-Ḥabḥāb sent Ḥabīb ibn Abī ʿUbayda, who raided a village in Sardinia and inflicted heavy losses in killing and capture.

Ibn al-Kalbī said: In it, Muʿāwiya ibn Hishām campaigned in the land of the Romans until he reached Sībara. His raiding parties reached Sardah, and they took captives. Khālid ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Ḥakam led the pilgrimage. In the year 117, ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Hurmuz al-Aʿraj, the client of Rabīʿa ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, died; Saʿīd ibn Yasār, the client of al-Ḥasan ibn ʿAlī, died; Muḥammad ibn Kaʿb al-Quraẓī died; and ʿĀʾisha daughter of Saʿd ibn Mālik and Sukayna daughter of al-Ḥusayn ibn ʿAlī all died in Medina. Qatāda ibn Diʿāma died at Wāsiṭ. In it, Muʿādh ibn Muʿādh was born.

The Year 118

In it, Marwān ibn Muḥammad campaigned from Armenia and entered the land of Wartanīs through three gates. Wartanīs fled to the Khazars and abandoned the citadel. Marwān set up mangonels against it. The people of Khamrīn killed Wartanīs and sent his head to Marwān. Marwān displayed Wartanīs’s head to the people of his citadel. They submitted to Marwān’s terms, and he killed the fighting men and took the children captive.

Abū Khālid said: In that year, Ibn al-Ḥabḥāb sent Qutham ibn ʿAwānah al-Kalbī on an expedition. He encountered an advance party from Sicily, which surrounded him and then let him go. Muʿāwiyah ibn Hishām raided the land of the Romans, and Muḥammad ibn Hishām ibn Ismāʿīl led the pilgrimage. In the year 118, Abū Jaʿfar Muḥammad ibn ʿAlī ibn Ḥusayn ibn ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib died in Medina; ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās in Syria; ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Sābiṭ al-Jumaḥī in Mecca; ʿAmr ibn Shuʿayb ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ in al-Ṭāʾif; ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Mulaykah in Mecca; ʿUbādah ibn Nasī in Syria; and ʿAmr ibn Murrah al-Jumalī in Kūfa. In that year, Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Anṣārī was born.

The Year 119

In that year, Marwān ibn Muḥammad raided from Armenia in the campaign called al-Sāʾiḥah. He entered through the Gate of al-Lān and traversed all the land of al-Lān until he left it for the land of the Khazars. He passed through Balanjar and Samandar and reached al-Bayḍāʾ, where the Khāqān resides. The Khāqān fled.

In that year, Ibn al-Ḥabḥāb also sent Qutham ibn ʿAwānah. He seized a fortress in Sardinia, in the lands of the Maghrib. Qutham drowned aboard one of the Muslims’ ships, while some of them survived. In that year, Muʿāwiyah ibn Hishām raided the land of the Romans and reached Blūniyah. Sulaymān ibn Hishām also raided the land of the Romans from the direction of al-Jazīrah.

In that year, ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān ibn Muḥammad killed Hazār Tarkhān and most of his companions in Armenia. Maslamah ibn Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, Abū Shākir, led the pilgrimage. In the year 119, Sulaymān ibn Mūsā died in Syria; Abū Maʿshar in Kūfa; Ḥabīb ibn Abī Thābit in Kūfa; and Qays ibn Saʿd and Iyās ibn Salamah ibn al-Akwaʿ in Medina.

And ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Saʿīd ibn Yarbuʿ in Medina, and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Wāqid ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb.

The Year 120

In that year, Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik dismissed Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Qasrī from Iraq and appointed Yūsuf ibn ʿUmar over it.

Muḥammad ibn Hishām ibn Ismāʿīl led the pilgrimage, and Maslamah ibn Hishām raided the land of the Romans. In the year 120, al-Jārūd ibn Abī Sabrah al-Hudhalī died in Baṣra; ʿĀṣim ibn ʿUmar ibn Qatādah al-Anṣārī in Medina; Abū Bakr ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAmr ibn Ḥazm in Medina; Ḥammād ibn Abī Sulaymān in Kūfa; ʿAdī ibn ʿAdī in al-Jazīrah; Asad ibn ʿAbd Allāh in Khurāsān; Maslamah ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān on Wednesday in al-Muḥarram, in Syria; Abū Qays ʿAmr ibn Muslim al-Bāhilī; and Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn al-Ḥārith al-Laythī. In that year, Yaḥyā ibn Saʿīd al-Qaṭṭān was born.

Abū Khālid said: Ifrīqiyah was not raided in the year 120. Sulaymān ibn Hishām raided on the summer campaign. In that year, Hishām ibn ʿUrwah came to Baṣra during the governorship of Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Qasrī.

Al-Walīd ibn Hishām narrated to us from his father, from his grandfather; and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mughīrah, from his father; and Abū al-Yaqẓān ʿĀmir ibn Ḥafṣ and others said: Iraq was placed under Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yazīd ibn Asad ibn Kurz al-Bajalī in the year 106, and he was dismissed in the year 120.

Whoever held Khālid al-Qasrī’s police post and was responsible for correspondence, taxation, and al-tajwīn held the police post at Wāsiṭ: ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd al-Aʿlā al-Ḥakamī; at Kūfa: al-ʿUryān ibn al-Haytham al-Nakhaʿī; and at Baṣra: Mālik ibn al-Mundhir ibn al-Jārūd al-ʿAbdī. Then he dismissed him and appointed Bilāl ibn Abī Burdah ibn Abī Mūsā al-Ashʿarī, and then added the prayer leadership and judgeship to his duties. Khālid’s secretary for correspondence was Dāwūd ibn Saʿīd al-Kātib; for taxation, al-Ḥajjāj ibn ʿUmayr; and for al-tajwīn, Hārūn ibn Miyyās, the client of Banū Layth.

Khālid was killed in the year 126, when he was about sixty years old. During Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh’s governorship of Iraq, which lasted about fourteen years, Nuʿaym ibn Abī Hind died at the beginning of Khālid’s governorship; and Shamir ibn ʿAṭiyyah at its beginning; ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn al-Aṣbahānī and al-ʿAyẓār ibn Ḥurayth, Abū al-Safar, both in its middle; Ḥumayd ibn Hilāl al-ʿAdawī, Anas ibn Sīrīn, al-Azraq ibn Qays, and ʿAmmār ibn Abī ʿAmmār, the client of Banū Hāshim, in the middle part of Khālid’s governorship; Muḥārib ibn Dithār al-Dhuhlī, al-Qāsim ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd, Wabrah ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maysarah, Abū ʿAwn al-Thaqafī, ʿAdī ibn Thābit, ʿAlqamah ibn Marthad, and ʿAwn ibn Abī Juḥayfah at the end of Khālid’s governorship; and ʿAṭiyyah ibn Saʿd al-ʿAwfī.

The Year 121

Marwān ibn Muḥammad’s campaign from Armenia

In that year, Marwān ibn Muḥammad, who was its governor, raided from Armenia. He came to the fortress of Bayt al-Sarīr, where he killed and took captives. Then he came to a second fortress, where he killed and took captives. He entered Ghumisk, a fortress containing the royal residence, where the golden throne was kept. Marwān remained there through a winter and a summer campaign. The king made peace with him, then fled until he reached a fortress called Khathraj. There was a throne there, with a tribute of one thousand heads each year

In that year, al-Baṭṭāl was killed in the land of the Romans. In the year 121, Muḥammad ibn Ibrāhīm al-Taymī, Muḥammad ibn Yaḥyā ibn Ḥibbān al-Anṣārī, and ʿĀmir ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr died. In that year, Abū ʿĀṣim al-Ḍaḥḥāk ibn Makhlad was born.

The Year 122

The revolt of ʿAbd al-Aʿlā ibn Ḥudayj

Abū Khālid said: In that year, ʿAbd al-Aʿlā ibn Ḥudayj, the client of Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr, revolted in Tangier

The revolt of Maysarah al-Ḥaqīr

In that year, Maysarah al-Ḥaqīr also revolted. He used to sell water in al-Qayrawān. The two revolts began by agreement on the middle day of Ramaḍān in the year 122. Ismāʿīl ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-Ḥabḥāb dispatched an army against Maysarah al-Ḥaqīr and his companions and routed them. Then Maysarah al-Ḥaqīr attacked Ismāʿīl ibn ʿUbayd Allāh’s camp by night, killing and taking captives. Then Maysarah al-Ḥaqīr sent out a commander, who killed ʿAbd al-Aʿlā ibn Ḥudayj. Ibn al-Ḥabḥāb learned that his son Ismāʿīl had been killed, and went out and met Maysarah at a river called the River of al-Kadar. Khālid ibn Abī Ḥabīb, Abū al-Aṣamm, commanded Ibn al-Ḥabḥāb’s forces. Khālid and his son were killed, as were ʿUthmān ibn Abī ʿUbaydah ibn ʿUqbah ibn Nāfiʿ and his son Ibrāhīm ibn ʿUthmān; Mūsā ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān; ʿAbd al-Karīm ibn Masḥal ibn ʿUqbah ibn Ḍirār ibn al-Khaṭṭāb; and Zarārah ibn ʿAmr, a descendant of Abū ʿAzīz ibn ʿUmayr, the brother of Muṣʿab ibn ʿUmayr, of Banū ʿAbd al-Dār ibn Quṣayy. The battle was therefore called the Battle of the Nobles. Their killing took place at the end of the year or in al-Muḥarram of the year 123.

Abū Khālid said: When Ibn al-Ḥabḥāb learned of their deaths, he appointed ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn al-Mughīrah al-ʿAbdī as governor of Tilimsān. He began killing the Ṣufrīs, so he was called “the Butcher.” They revolted against ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn al-Mughīrah, who withdrew. Ḥabīb ibn Abī ʿUbaydah returned from his raid at sea, and Ibn al-Ḥabḥāb dispatched him. He encamped by the Wādī of Tilimsān and did not cross it until Ibn al-Ḥabḥāb’s governorship ended.

In that year, Zayd ibn Ḥusayn ibn ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib was killed in Kūfa.

Abū al-Yaqẓān related to me, saying: In that year, Muʿāwiyah ibn Hishām raided the land of the Romans, and Sulaymān ibn Hishām also raided. Together they besieged the Romans. The Muslims suffered severe hunger and high prices

In that year, al-Ḥakam ibn ʿAwānah was struck down in al-Sind. Yūsuf ibn ʿUmar appointed ʿAmr ibn Muḥammad ibn al-Qāsim in his place. Muḥammad ibn Hishām raided on the summer campaign, and Muḥammad ibn Hishām ibn Ismāʿīl led the pilgrimage. In that year, Yazīd ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Qusayṭ died in Medina; Iyās ibn Muʿāwiyah ibn Qurrah al-Muzanī at Wāsiṭ; Zubayd ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Karīm al-Ayyāmī; Bukayr ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Ashajj; and Salamah ibn Kuhayl in Kūfa; and Yaʿqūb ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Ashajj.

In that year, Muḥammad ibn Sulaymān ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib was born at al-Ḥumaymah in the land of Syria.

The Year 123

In that year, Kulthūm ibn ʿIyāḍ arrived as governor of Ifrīqiyah at the beginning of Shaʿbān. He traveled until he encamped at Tilimsān. Yazīd ibn Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, who was called al-Afḳam, led the pilgrimage.

Sulaymān ibn Hishām raided on the summer campaign. In that year, al-Zuhrī performed the pilgrimage.

The Year 124

In that year, Maysarah al-Ḥaqīr al-Ṣufrī died in the lands of the Maghrib, and the Ṣufrīs split into two groups: one led by Khālid ibn Ḥumayd, and one led by Sālim Abū Yūsuf al-Azdī. Kulthūm ibn ʿIyāḍ marched against them. They all gathered and met Kulthūm ibn ʿIyāḍ at a valley among the valleys of Tangier

Kulthūm, Muḥammad ibn ʿUbayd Allāh al-Azdī, Yazīd ibn Saʿīd ibn ʿAmr al-Ḥarashī, and Ḥabīb ibn Abī ʿUbaydah were killed. They plundered Kulthūm’s camp and took the dependents captive. Bulj ibn Bishr, Kulthūm’s cousin, fled with the people, and Abū Yūsuf ibn Ḥumayd pursued them. Ḥassān ibn ʿAnābah was in Bulj ibn Bishr’s rearguard. When he caught up with them, he fought them and stood firm against them, routing them. Abū Yūsuf and many of the Ṣufrīs were killed. The Ṣufrīs continued in flight, while Bulj and his companions went on and entered the fortress. They appointed Abū al-Khaṭṭār al-Kalbī over the people and called them to arms, but the people refused and said, “Appoint ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿUqbah al-Ghifārī.” So he appointed him. He met ʿUkāshah al-Fazārī at al-Faḥṣ al-Abyaḍ. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān routed him and killed many of the Berbers. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān went on and encamped in the Zāb, where he fasted for the month of Ramaḍān. Al-Fazārī fled to Tangier, where ʿAbd al-Wāḥid ibn Yazīd al-Hawwārī met him. Khālid ibn Ḥumayd, the leader of the Ṣufrīs, had sent al-Hawwārī to fight the people of Ifrīqiyah, and he brought al-Fazārī back with him. Ḥanẓalah wrote to ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿUqbah ibn Nāfiʿ, ordering him to meet ʿAbd al-Wāḥid ibn Yazīd. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān set out with the people of the Zāb, and they met on Thursday, the middle day of Dhū al-Qaʿdah in the year 124. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿUqbah, Marwān ibn ʿUthmān al-Ghassānī, and Muḥammad ibn Yūsuf Fī Bishr were killed. The remnants of the army arrived at al-Qayrawān under Ḥanẓalah, and ʿAbd al-Wāḥid took control of the families of the people of Ṭubnah. Ḥanẓalah appointed Thābit ibn Khaytham. On the first day of Ṣafar in the year 125, ʿAbd al-Wāḥid advanced. They met, Ibn Khaytham was killed, and the people fled. Ḥanẓalah wrote to al-Mustanīr ibn al-Ḥārith al-Ḥarashī, his governor in Tunis: “If you are able to fight the people, then do so; otherwise, come here.” He came, bringing the families with him. Khālid ibn Ḥumayd, the leader of the Ṣufrīs in Tangier, learned that ʿAbd al-Wāḥid had been hailed as caliph. He sent ʿAbd al-Aʿlā Zarzar, the client of Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr, with cavalry and ordered him to take down ʿAbd al-Wāḥid’s banner and appointed Zarzar over his companions. ʿAbd al-Wāḥid learned of this and set out for al-Qayrawān. Ḥanẓalah’s cavalry met them, ʿAbd al-Wāḥid was killed, and the Berbers were routed. A great number of them were killed. Ḥanẓalah’s herald proclaimed safe-conduct. ʿUkāshah al-Fazārī fled, but some people seized him, bound him tightly, and sent him to Ḥanẓalah. They asked for safe-conduct, but Ḥanẓalah killed him. The Ṣufrīs fled, and the land became peaceful.

In the year 124, the Berbers in al-Andalus revolted, and ʿUkāshah ibn Ayyūb al-Fazārī revolted at Qābis in Ifrīqiyah, pursuing an aim that was not in accord with what was being called for. At that time, the governor of Ifrīqiyah was ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿUqbah al-Ghifārī, Kulthūm’s deputy. He dispatched Muslim ibn Sawādah al-Fihrī, but ʿUkāshah routed him until he entered al-Qayrawān. ʿUkāshah went on to the city of Qābis, where ʿAbd al-Aʿlā ibn ʿUqbah and Saʿīd ibn Bajrah al-Ghassānī were. He besieged them and set up mangonels against it, but did not reach the two of them. He went on to Qafṣah and besieged it. The remnants of Kulthūm’s army arrived to join ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿUqbah. He rose with them and left ʿAbd al-Ḥamīd ibn Dhuʾayb al-Sahmī as his deputy in al-Qayrawān. ʿUkāshah advanced toward him, and they met in Ṣafar of the year 124. ʿUkāshah al-Fazārī was routed, reached Ṭubnah, and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān returned to al-Qayrawān. Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik appointed Ḥanẓalah ibn Ṣafwān al-Kalbī, who arrived there in the middle of Jumādā I of the year 124.

Muḥammad ibn Hishām ibn Ismāʿīl led the pilgrimage.

In the year 124, Muḥammad ibn Muslim ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Shihāb al-Zuhrī died on Tuesday night, seventeen nights having passed of Ramaḍān; Muḥammad ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib in Syria; al-Qāsim ibn Abī Bazzah in Mecca; Abū Jamrah al-Ḍubaʿī in Baṣra; Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, a descendant of Saʿd ibn Zurārah, in Medina; and, at the end of Hishām’s governorship, ʿĀmir ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām died.

Chapter 14

The caliphate of al-Walīd ibn Yazīd

The Year 125

In that year, Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān ﵀—died at al-Ruṣāfah.

Al-Walīd ibn Hishām narrated to me from his father, from his grandfather; and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mughīrah, from his father; and Abū al-Yaqẓān and others said: Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik died at al-Ruṣāfah on Wednesday, when three nights had passed

The governors appointed by Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik

Mecca, Medina, and Ṭāʾif: Muḥammad ibn Hishām ibn Ismāʿīl al-Makhzūmī, in the year 106, in Jumādā I. He continued as governor of Mecca until Hishām died.

Medina: Hishām assigned it, together with Mecca, to Muḥammad ibn Hishām ibn Ismāʿīl. Then he dismissed him in the year 114 and appointed Khālid ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Ḥakam. Then he dismissed him in the year 119 and wrote to Abū Bakr ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAmr ibn Ḥazm, who led the people in prayer until Muḥammad ibn Ibrāhīm ibn Hishām arrived in the year 119. He continued as governor until Hishām died.

Yemen: Hishām appointed Yūsuf ibn ʿUmar al-Thaqafī. He arrived there with three nights of Ramaḍān remaining in the year 106 and continued as governor until Hishām wrote to him in the year 120 appointing him over Iraq. He set out, leaving his son al-Ṣalt ibn Yūsuf as his deputy. Then he appointed his brother al-Qāsim ibn ʿUmar over it, and he continued as governor until Hishām died.

Baṣra: When Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Qasrī took charge of Iraq, he appointed Abān ibn Ḍubāra ibn ʿUfayr ibn Sayf ibn Dhī Yazan, one of the people of Ḥimṣ.

The police: ʿUqba ibn ʿAbd al-Aʿlā al-Kilāʿī, one of the people of Damascus. Then he appointed Mālik ibn al-Mundhir ibn al-Jārūd al-ʿAbdī over the police. He arrived there in Dhū al-Qaʿda of the year 106. Then he dismissed Mālik ibn al-Mundhir ibn al-Jārūd and appointed Bilāl ibn Abī Burda for a year. Then he appointed al-Naḍr ibn ʿUmar al-Muqriʾ al-Ḥimyarī, one of the people of Damascus, to lead the prayers. Then he dismissed him at the end of the year 110 and combined the prayer leadership, police, and judgeship under Bilāl ibn Abī Burda until Khālid was removed from Iraq in the year 120 and Yūsuf ibn ʿUmar al-Thaqafī was appointed over Iraq. Yūsuf sent al-Wāziʿ ibn ʿAbbād al-Kalbī, who seized Bilāl. Then Yūsuf appointed Kathīr ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Sulamī, whose kunya was Abū al-ʿĀj. Then he dismissed him in the year 122 and appointed al-Qāsim ibn Muḥammad, who continued as governor until Hishām died.

Kūfa: Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh appointed ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Juzʾ ibn Ḥadrajān al-Azdī, one of the people of Palestine, over it. Then he dismissed him and appointed Ismāʿīl ibn Awsaṭ al-Bajalī. Then he dismissed him and appointed ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr al-Bajalī. Then he dismissed him and appointed his brother ʿĀṣim ibn ʿAmr. Then he dismissed him and appointed Ḍubays ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Bajalī. Then he dismissed him and appointed Nawf al-Ashʿarī. Then he dismissed him and appointed Ziyād ibn ʿUbayd Allāh al-Ḥārithī. Then Khālid was dismissed in the year 120 and Yūsuf ibn ʿUmar was appointed. Yūsuf appointed al-Ḥakam ibn al-Ṣalt al-Thaqafī, then dismissed him and appointed Yūsuf ibn Muḥammad ibn al-Qāsim al-Thaqafī. Then he dismissed him and appointed Muḥammad ibn ʿUbayd Allāh al-Thaqafī. Then he dismissed him and appointed Ziyād ibn Ṣakhr al-Lakhmī. Then he dismissed him and appointed ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās al-Kindī. Then he dismissed him and appointed Abū Umayya ibn al-Mughīra ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī ʿAqīl al-Thaqafī. He led the Friday prayer for one week, until Yūsuf ibn ʿUmar fled.

Khurāsān: Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh appointed his brother Asad ibn ʿAbd Allāh over Khurāsān. Then Hishām dismissed him in the year 108 and appointed Ashras ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Sulamī. Then he dismissed him in the year 113 and appointed al-Junayd ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, of Murra of Ghatafān. Then he dismissed him in the year 115 and appointed ʿĀṣim ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yazīd al-Hilālī. Then it was reunited under Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh for a second term, and he appointed his brother Asad ibn ʿAbd Allāh. Asad died in the year 120, shortly before Khālid was dismissed, and appointed Jaʿfar ibn Ḥanẓala al-Bahrānī as his deputy. Then Hishām appointed Naṣr ibn Sayyār al-Laythī, who remained in office until Hishām died.

Sijistān: Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh appointed Yazīd ibn al-Ghurayf al-Hamadhānī over it, then al-Aṣfaḥ al-Kindī, Abū Khālid ibn al-Aṣfaḥ al-Kindī, then ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Burda ibn Abī Mūsā al-Ashʿarī. He continued as governor until Khālid was dismissed and Yūsuf ibn ʿUmar was appointed over Iraq. Yūsuf appointed Muḥammad ibn Ḥujr ibn Qays al-ʿAbdī over it, then Ibrāhīm ibn ʿĀṣim al-ʿUqaylī. When Ibrāhīm died, Yūsuf appointed Ḥarb ibn Quṭn ibn Qabīṣa ibn Mukhāriq al-Hilālī. He continued as governor until Hishām died.

Sind: Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh kept al-Junayd ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, of Murra of Ghatafān, in office there for two years. Then he dismissed him and appointed Tamīm ibn Zayd al-Qaynī. Then he dismissed him and appointed al-Ḥakam ibn ʿAwāna. Al-Maydh killed al-Ḥakam, and he appointed Muḥammad ibn ʿArrār al-Kalbī as his deputy. Yūsuf dismissed him in the year 122 and appointed ʿAmr ibn Muḥammad ibn al-Qāsim, who continued as governor until Hishām died.

The Bahrain: Khālid’s officials there were Muḥammad ibn Ziyād ibn Jarīr ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Bajalī, Hazzān ibn Saʿīd, Yaḥyā ibn Ismāʿīl, and Yaḥyā ibn Ziyād ibn al-Ḥārith al-Ḥārithī.

Yūsuf’s officials over the two Bahrains were ʿAbd Allāh ibn Sharik al-Numayrī and Muḥammad ibn Ḥassān ibn Saʿd al-Usaydī. Al-Musayyib ibn Faḍāla gained control over it for about three years.

Al-Yamāma: Hishām appointed al-Muhājir ibn ʿAbd Allāh, of Banū Bakr ibn Kilāb. When al-Muhājir died, he appointed his son over it, and he remained there until al-Walīd was killed.

Egypt: Hishām appointed Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān over it. Then he appointed ʿUbayda ibn al-Ḥabḥāb, the client of Banū Sulūl.

Ifrīqiya: Bishr ibn Ṣafwān al-Kalbī was in charge there. He left it as an envoy to Yazīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, appointing Yaḥyā ibn Māʿiṣa al-Kalbī as his deputy. Hishām sent Bishr ibn Ṣafwān back there, and he arrived in the year 106. He continued as governor until he died in the year 109, appointing Nuʿās ibn Qurṭ al-Kalbī as his deputy. Hishām dismissed him and appointed ʿUbayda ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Sulamī. He arrived there in the year 110, then departed

The pilgrimage season: In the year 105, Hishām ibn Ismāʿīl al-Makhzūmī; in the year 106, Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik; in the years 107, 108, 109, 110, 111, and 112, Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik; in the year 113, Ibrāhīm ibn Hishām ibn Ismāʿīl; in the year 114, Sulaymān ibn Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik; in the year 115, Khālid ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Ḥakam ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ; in the year 116, Muḥammad ibn Hishām ibn Ismāʿīl; in the year 117, al-Walīd ibn Yazīd. Al-Walīd ibn Hishām and Abū al-Yaqẓān reported to me that ʿĪsā ibn Miqsam, the client of al-Walīd ibn Yazīd, led the pilgrimage in the year 116. In the year 117, Khālid ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Ḥakam; in the year 118, Muḥammad ibn Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik; in the year 119, Muslim ibn Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik; in the years 120, 121, and 122, Muḥammad ibn Hishām ibn Ismāʿīl; in the year 123, Yazīd ibn Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik; in the year 124, Muḥammad ibn Hishām ibn Ismāʿīl.

The summer campaign: Muslim ibn ʿAbd al-Malik appointed his two sons, Muʿāwiya and Sulaymān, the sons of Hishām, over it. We have explained this in the chronological years.

Armenia: When Yazīd died, al-Jarrāḥ ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Ḥakamī was in charge there, and Hishām confirmed him in office. Then he dismissed him in the year 107 and appointed Maslama ibn ʿAbd al-Malik. Then he dismissed him in the year 109 and appointed al-Jarrāḥ for a second term. Al-Jarrāḥ was killed in the year 112, and Saʿīd ibn ʿAmr al-Ḥarashī was appointed over it. Then he dismissed him in the year 113 and appointed Maslama ibn ʿAbd al-Malik. When Maslama departed, he appointed Marwān ibn Muḥammad as his deputy. Marwān ibn Muḥammad took charge of it at the beginning of the year 114.

The judges during the rule of Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik

Baṣra: Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh appointed Thumāma ibn Anas ibn Mālik as judge of Baṣra. Then he dismissed him in the year 109 and assigned the judgeship to Bilāl ibn Abī Burda. He continued as judge until Yūsuf ibn ʿUmar arrived in the year 120 and appointed ʿAbd Allāh ibn Burayda al-Aslamī. The latter soon died, so Yūsuf appointed ʿĀmir ibn ʿUbayda al-Bāhilī as judge. He continued as judge until Hishām and al-Walīd died and the civil strife broke out; then he withdrew.

Kūfa: Khālid confirmed al-Ḥusayn ibn al-Ḥasan al-Kindī in office there. Then he dismissed him; then came Saʿīd ibn Ashwaʿ al-Hamdānī; then Muḥārib ibn Dithār in the year 113; then al-Ḥakam ibn ʿUtayba al-ʿIjlī. Then he reinstated Ibn Ashwaʿ, who remained judge until he died. Then he appointed ʿĪsā ibn al-Musayyib al-Bajalī. Then Yūsuf ibn ʿUmar arrived, dismissed ʿĪsā ibn al-Musayyib, and appointed ʿAbd Allāh ibn Shubrumah al-Ḍabbī. Then he dismissed him, appointed him over the treasury, and appointed Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Abī Laylā. He remained judge until Hishām and al-Walīd died.

Medina: Hishām appointed Ibrāhīm ibn Hishām ibn Ismāʿīl over Medina, and he appointed Muḥammad ibn Ṣafwān al-Jumaḥī as judge. Then al-Ṣalt ibn Zubayd ibn al-Ṣalt was appointed judge. Then Ibrāhīm ibn Hishām was dismissed in the year 114, and Khālid ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Ḥakam ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ was appointed over Medina. Khālid appointed Abū Bakr ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥuwayṭib, of Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy, as judge. Then he dismissed him and appointed Muḥammad ibn Ṣafwān al-Jumaḥī as judge. Then Hishām dismissed Khālid in the year 119 and wrote to Abū Bakr ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAmr ibn Ḥazm, who continued as judge until he died.

Hishām’s police: He retained Kaʿb ibn Ḥāmid al-ʿAbsī for thirteen years, then appointed him over Armenia. He appointed Yazīd ibn Yaʿlā ibn Ḍakhm al-ʿAbsī over the police.

The secretary of correspondence: Sālim, the client of Saʿīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik.

The land tax and the army: Usāma ibn Zayd. Then he dismissed him and appointed ʿUbayda ibn al-Ḥabḥāb, the client of Banū Sulūl. Then he appointed him over Egypt and put Saʿīd ibn ʿUqba, the client of Banū al-Ḥārith ibn Kaʿb, in his place.

The seal: al-Rabīʿ ibn Shābūr, the client of Banū al-Ḥarīsh.

The treasuries and the public funds: ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn al-Ḥārith.

The guard: Naṣīr, his client. Then he dismissed him and appointed al-Rabīʿ ibn Ziyād, also over the seal.

The small seal and the private retinue: Iṣṭakhr, Abū al-Zubayr, his client.

His chamberlain: Ghālib ibn Masʿūd, his client.

In the year 125, al-Walīd ibn Yazīd wrote to Yūsuf ibn ʿUmar. Yūsuf came to him, and he handed over to him Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Qasrī and Muḥammad and Ibrāhīm, the sons of Hishām ibn Ismāʿīl al-Makhzūmī. He ordered him to kill them.

Ismāʿīl ibn Ibrāhīm al-Shaʿīrāwī al-ʿAtakī reported to me: al-Sirrī ibn Muslim Abū Bishr ibn al-Sirrī reported to me: “I saw them when Yūsuf ibn ʿUmar brought them to al-Ḥīra, and Khālid was in a cloak, in one side of a litter. He tortured them until he killed them.”

In that year, al-Ghamr ibn Yazīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik raided in the summer campaign, and Yūsuf ibn ʿUmar led the pilgrimage.

In that year, Ṣāliḥ ibn Nabhān, the client of al-Tawʾama, daughter of Umayya ibn Khalaf, died in Medina; Abū Bishr Jaʿfar ibn Abī Waḥshiyya died in Wāsiṭ; Budayl ibn Maysara al-ʿUqaylī died in Baṣra; Ādam ibn ʿAlī al-Shaybānī died in Kūfa; and Muḥammad ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAṭāʾ died in Medina.

During Yūsuf ibn ʿUmar’s governorship of Iraq, Zubayd al-Ayyāmī, Simāk ibn Ḥarb al-Dhuhlī, Jabala ibn Suḥaym al-Shaybānī, and Ashʿath ibn Abī al-Shaʿthāʾ died.

Chapter 15

The end of the Umayyads: Yazīd ibn al-Walīd and Marwān ibn Muḥammad

The Year 126

In it, al-Walīd ibn Yazīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān was killed.

Al-Walīd ibn Hishām reported to me, from his father, from his grandfather; ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mughīra reported from his father; and Abū al-Yaqẓān and others said: “Al-Walīd was killed at al-Bakhraʾ, near Tadmur, several miles away, on Thursday, with two nights remaining in Jumādā II of the year 126. He was thirty-five or thirty-six years old.”

Yaḥyā ibn Muḥammad reported to me, from ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Abī ʿImrān, who said: “He was killed at the age of forty-five or forty-four.”

Ḥātim ibn Muslim said: “He was forty-five years old and some months.”

Al-Walīd was born in Damascus in the year 90; it is also said, in the year 92. His rule lasted one year, two months, and twenty-two days.

Ismāʿīl ibn Ibrāhīm reported to me: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Wāqid al-Jarmī reported to me—he had witnessed al-Walīd’s killing—and said: “When they resolved to kill al-Walīd, they entrusted their affair to Yazīd ibn al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān. Among his kinsmen, ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn al-Ḥajjāj ibn ʿAbd al-Malik swore allegiance to him. Yazīd ibn al-Walīd set out and came to his brother al-ʿAbbās at night. He consulted him about killing al-Walīd, but al-ʿAbbās forbade him to do so. Yazīd went on at night until he entered Damascus with forty men. They broke the door of the private enclosure and entered upon its governor, bound him, and carried the funds on carts to the gate of the racecourse. He appointed ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn

News reached al-Walīd ibn Yazīd, and he set out from al-Balqāʾ toward Ḥimṣ. He wrote to al-ʿAbbās ibn al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, asking him to come with a force from among the people of Ḥimṣ; al-ʿAbbās was nearby. Al-Walīd set out until he reached al-Bakhraʾ, a palace in the wilderness, and the sandy tract of Tadmur, several miles away. The cavalry came upon al-Walīd at al-Bakhraʾ. Al-ʿAbbās ibn al-Walīd arrived without cavalry, so ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn al-Ḥajjāj detained him behind him. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz’s herald called out: ‘Whoever comes to al-ʿAbbās ibn al-Walīd is safe. He is between us and you.’ The people thought that al-ʿAbbās was with ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, so they deserted al-Walīd. The people rushed upon him. The first to rush upon him were al-Sirrī ibn Ziyād ibn Abī Kabsha al-Saksakī and ʿAbd al-Salām al-Lakhmī. Al-Sirrī swung his sword at him, ʿAbd al-Salām struck him on the temple, and he was killed.

Ismāʿīl said: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Wāqid al-Jarmī reported to me: “They entered upon al-Walīd when he had put on two coats of mail, one over the other, and had a drawn sword in his hand. They hung back from him, so their herald called out: ‘Kill the sodomite with the killing of the people of Lot!’ Then he was killed.”

Ismāʿīl said: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Wāqid reported to me: Yazīd ibn Abī Farwa, the client of Banū Umayya, reported to me: “When Yazīd ibn al-Walīd was brought the head of al-Walīd ibn Yazīd, he said: ‘Display it to the people.’ I said: ‘Do not do that. Only the head of a Kharijite is displayed.’ He swore that he would display it, and that no one but me would display it. It was placed on a spear and displayed on the steps of the mosque of Damascus. Then he said: ‘Go and carry it around the city of Damascus.’”

Al-Walīd ibn Hishām related to me, from his father, who said: When they surrounded al-Walīd, he took the Qur’an and said, “I am being killed just as my cousin ʿUthmān was killed.”

Then Ismāʿīl ibn Ibrāhīm related to me, saying: Ibrāhīm ibn Isḥāq related to me that Yazīd ibn al-Walīd rose to preach. He praised God and extolled Him, then said: “To proceed: People, by God, I did not rise in rebellion out of insolence, arrogance, eagerness for this world, or desire for kingship. I do not seek to exalt myself or declare my deeds pure. I am indeed unjust to myself if my Lord does not have mercy on me. Rather, I rose in anger for God and His religion, calling to His Book and the Sunnah of His Prophet, when the signs of guidance had been effaced, the light of the people of piety extinguished, and the obstinate tyrant had appeared—one who deems inviolable things lawful, follows innovation, and alters the Sunnah. When I saw that, I feared for you, lest darkness envelop you and not lift, given the multitude of your sins and the hardness of your hearts. I feared that many people would call others to what he was doing and that some of you who answered them would respond. So I sought God’s guidance in my affair, asking Him not to leave me to myself, and I called to this cause those of my family and people under my authority who answered me. He was my cousin by lineage and my equal in standing. God relieved the servants of him and purified the lands of him, by God’s authority and aid, without any power or strength of ours, but through God’s power, strength, authority, and aid. People, if I am entrusted with your affairs, I pledge you that I will not lay one brick upon another or one stone upon another, nor transfer wealth from one region to another, until I have secured its frontier and allocated to its garrisons what they need to be strong. If anything remains, I will return it to the region next to it that needs it more, until the livelihood of the Muslims is sound and you are equal in it. I will not keep your troops deployed away from home, lest you and your families be put to trial. If you wish to give me your allegiance on the terms I have offered, I will uphold them for you. If I deviate, then you owe me no allegiance. If you see someone better able than I to undertake this, and wish to pledge allegiance to him, I will be the first to pledge allegiance and enter into his obedience. I say these words of mine, and I ask God’s forgiveness for myself and for you.”

The names of the officials appointed by al-Walīd ibn Yazīd

Medina: Al-Walīd wrote to Muḥammad ibn Hishām ibn Ismāʿīl, who was governor over Mecca for Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik. He came to him and appointed Muḥammad ibn Abī Bakr ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAmr ibn Ḥazm as his deputy over Medina. Al-Walīd dismissed him and consolidated Medina, Mecca, and Ṭāʾif under Yūsuf ibn Muḥammad ibn Yūsuf, until al-Walīd was killed.

Yemen: Al-Ḍaḥḥāk ibn Zaml, until al-Walīd was killed.

Baṣra: Al-Qāsim ibn Muḥammad ibn al-Qāsim was in charge there until Hishām died. Al-Walīd confirmed him in office until he was killed.

Kūfa: ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās al-Kindī; then Yūsuf dismissed him and appointed Abū Umayya ibn al-Mughīra ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī ʿAqīl al-Thaqafī. He remained for one Friday prayer, until Yūsuf fled after al-Walīd was killed.

Khurāsān: Naṣr ibn Sayyār al-Laythī was confirmed in office there, until al-Walīd was killed.

Sijistān: Ḥarb ibn Qaṭan ibn Qubayṣa al-Hilālī, until al-Walīd was killed.

Sind: ʿAmr ibn Muḥammad ibn al-Qāsim al-Thaqafī, until al-Walīd was killed.

Al-Baḥrayn: Muḥammad ibn Ḥassān ibn Saʿīd al-Asadī, until al-Walīd was killed. It is also said that Bishr ibn Salām al-ʿAbdī killed al-Musayyib ibn Faḍāla and remained in office until Ibn Hubayra arrived.

Al-Yamāma: Al-Muhājir ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Kilābī, until al-Walīd was killed.

Ifrīqiya: Hishām died while Ḥanẓala ibn Ṣafwān was in charge there. He continued as governor until al-Walīd was killed, and departed in the year 109.

Oman: Yūsuf ibn ʿUmar appointed al-Fayḍ ibn Muḥammad ibn Kardam ibn Bayhas over it.

The judiciary

Judgeship of Baṣra: ʿĀmir ibn ʿUbayda, until al-Walīd was killed and the civil strife broke out; then he withdrew.

Kūfa: Ibn Abī Laylā, until al-Walīd was killed.

Medina: Yūsuf ibn Muḥammad ibn Yūsuf appointed Saʿd ibn Ibrāhīm; then he dismissed him and appointed Yaḥyā ibn Saʿd, until al-Walīd was killed.

The pilgrimage season: Yūsuf ibn Muḥammad ibn Yūsuf, in the year 125.

Al-Jazīra, Armenia, and Azerbaijan: Marwān ibn Muḥammad ibn Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam, until al-Walīd was killed. Marwān appointed ʿĀṣim ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yazīd al-Hilālī over Armenia and Azerbaijan, and departed for Syria.

The summer campaign: Al-Ghamr ibn Yazīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān.

Al-Walīd’s police chief: ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥanbal al-Kalbī; then he dismissed him and appointed ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿĀmir al-Kilāʿī.

The letter-writer: Sālim, client of Saʿīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik. Then his son ʿAbd Allāh ibn Sālim wrote letters for him.

Tax and army administration: ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Muḥammad ibn al-Ḥajjāj ibn Yūsuf; then al-Ḥajjāj appointed Ibn ʿUmayr.

The seal, treasuries, and public treasuries: ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥanbal al-Kalbī, in addition to the police office.

The small seal: Rabāḥ ibn Abī ʿAmāra.

His chamberlain: ʿĪsā ibn Miqsim. The guard: Ghaylān, the father-in-law of Abū Maʿn.

The governorship of Yūsuf ibn ʿUmar over Iraq

Al-Walīd ibn Hishām related to me, from his father, from his grandfather; and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mughīra, from his father; and Abū al-Yaqẓān and others said: Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān consolidated Iraq under Yūsuf ibn ʿUmar al-Thaqafī in the year 120. In al-Ḥīra, al-ʿAbbās ibn Saʿd ibn Murra, of Murra Ghatafān, was in charge of his police. He assigned the police of Baṣra and Kūfa to their governors, who appointed whomever they wished.

His tax secretary was Qaḥdham ibn Sulaymān, client of the family of Bakra; Rashdīn, his client, was in charge of the caliph’s correspondence; and ʿUqba was in charge of the governors’ correspondence. Yūsuf was killed in the year 127, aged a little over sixty. During Yūsuf ibn ʿUmar’s governorship, Jabala ibn Suḥaym died. In this year—the year 126—ʿAmr ibn Dīnār, client of the family of Bādhān, died in Mecca; Saʿīd ibn Abī Saʿīd al-Maqburī died in Medina; ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn al-Qāsim ibn Muḥammad ibn Abī Bakr died in Medina; and Sulaymān ibn Ḥabīb died in Syria. He had been a judge.

During the governorship of al-Walīd ibn Yazīd, Nabīh ibn Wahb died, as did Muḥammad ibn Qays, client of Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥarb, and al-Ḥuṣayn ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAmr ibn Saʿd ibn Muʿādh.

The allegiance to Yazīd ibn al-Walīd and his death

In it, Yazīd ibn al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik was given the oath of allegiance at the beginning of Rajab. His mother was the daughter of Yazdegerd, son of Kisrā.

In this year Yazīd ibn al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān, who was called Yazīd al-Nāqiṣ, died.

Al-Walīd ibn Hishām related to me, from his father, from his grandfather; ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mughīra, from his father; and Abū al-Yaqẓān and others said: Yazīd ibn al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik died in Damascus with ten nights remaining in Dhū al-Ḥijja, in the year 126, aged thirty-five or thirty-six. His brother Ibrāhīm ibn al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik performed the funeral prayer over him.

Ḥātim ibn Muslim said: He was forty-six years old.

ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz said: He was given the oath of allegiance at the age of thirty-nine, and died before reaching forty. Yazīd was born in Damascus in the year 96. The people of Syria pledged allegiance to Ibrāhīm ibn al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, except the people of Ḥimṣ, who refused to pledge allegiance to him.

Then al-ʿAlāʾ ibn Bard ibn Sinān related to me, saying: My father related to me: I was present with Yazīd ibn al-Walīd when he was near death. Qaṭan came to him and said, “I am a messenger from those behind your door. They adjure you by God to entrust their affairs to your brother Ibrāhīm.” He frowned and put his hand to his forehead, saying, “Am I to appoint Ibrāhīm?” Then he said to me, “Abū al-ʿAlāʾ, whom do you think I should designate as successor?” I replied, “I warned you against entering into this at the outset, so I will not advise you at its end.” He then suffered a fainting spell, until I thought he had died. This happened more than once. Qaṭan sat down and forged a document purporting to be Yazīd ibn al-Walīd’s designation, then summoned people and had them witness it. My father said: By God, Yazīd designated no one—not him, nor anyone else.

The names of the officials appointed by Yazīd ibn al-Walīd

He appointed Manṣūr ibn Jumhūr al-Kalbī over Iraq. It is said that he forged a designation in Yazīd’s name. He governed for about forty days and appointed the jurist al-Ḥajjāj ibn Arṭāʾa as his police chief.

Yazīd ibn al-Walīd appointed ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān over Mecca, Medina, and Ṭāʾif. Then he dismissed him and appointed ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz over them.

Baṣra: When al-Walīd was killed, Muḥammad ibn al-Qāsim ibn Muḥammad was in charge there and fled. The people of Baṣra came to an agreement on ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Umayya, known as al-Afwah, who led the Friday prayer there. Then Jarīr ibn Yazīd ibn Jarīr ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Bajalī arrived. After that, when ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz was appointed over Iraq, he wrote to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī ʿUthmān, who led the people in prayer until Ibn Suhayl arrived. It is also said that, after ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī ʿUthmān, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar appointed Saʿīd ibn ʿAmr ibn Jaʿda ibn Hubayra al-Makhzūmī. The people of Baṣra expelled him, so he appointed ʿAmr ibn Suhayl ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān. He dispatched al-Ḍaḥḥāk ibn Qays al-Khārijī when ʿAmāra had gained the upper hand.

Kūfa: Manṣūr ibn Jumhūr appointed ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās. Ibn ʿUmar dismissed him and appointed his brother, ʿĀṣim ibn ʿUmar.

Sijistān: When al-Walīd was killed, Ḥarb ibn Qaṭan was in charge there. Manṣūr ibn Jumhūr appointed Muḥammad ibn ʿAzzār over it. Ibn ʿUmar dismissed him and appointed Ḥarb ibn Qaṭan. He remained for a month, then left it, appointing Sawwār ibn al-Ashʿar al-Māzinī as his deputy. Bakr ibn Wāʾil were not satisfied, and they fought Tamīm. Ibn ʿUmar sent Saʿīd ibn ʿAmr, from the family of Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ, but Tamīm and Bakr were not satisfied.

Khurāsān: Naṣr ibn Sayyār, until the rule of the Umayyads came to an end.

Sind: When Manṣūr ibn Jumhūr was dismissed from Iraq, he went to Sind, seized control of it, and encamped there. He named it al-Manṣūriyya.

Ifrīqiya: ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥabīb seized control of it.

The judges

Judgeship of Baṣra: ʿĀmir ibn ʿUbayda withdrew during the civil strife.

Kūfa: Ibn Abī Laylā.

Medina: ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿUthmān appointed Saʿd ibn Ibrāhīm. Then Yazīd dismissed ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn ʿAbd Allāh and appointed ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, who dismissed Saʿd ibn Ibrāhīm and appointed ʿUthmān ibn ʿUmar al-Taymī as judge.

Yazīd ibn al-Walīd’s police chief: Bukayr ibn Shimākh al-Lakhmī, until Yazīd died.

The letter-writer: Layth ibn Abī Sulaymān ibn Saʿd.

Tax, the army, and the small seal: Al-Naḍr ibn ʿAmr, from the people of Yemen, in addition to the guard.

The seal of the caliphate: ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥanbal al-Kalbī; it is also said that it was his client Qaṭan. In this year, the year 126, ʿAmr ibn Suhayl ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam was appointed.

Abū ʿUbayda related to us, saying: When Yazīd ibn al-Walīd took office, he sent ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz to Iraq.

The revolt of Saʿīd ibn Baḥdal in al-Jazīra

In this year—the year 126—Saʿīd ibn Baḥdal set out from al-Namrayn of Qāsiṭ in al-Jazīra, in Shaʿbān of the year 126. Then he crossed the Tigris to Qardā; then…

He marched until he alighted at al-Marj, in the districts of Mosul, on the first day of the month of Ramaḍān. There he met Abū Karb, a man from Ḥimyar who had previously risen with a large number of people and been called Commander of the Faithful. They examined the dates of their respective risings and found that Saʿīd had risen first. Abū Karb acknowledged that precedence to him, yielded the command to him, went to his home, and his companions dispersed. About five hundred men gathered around Saʿīd, until he encamped outside the city of Mosul for several days. Its people asked him to leave them and gave him assurances of their agreement, so he departed from them and set out for Shahrazūr. There he met Shaybān ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz al-Yashkurī, around whom people had gathered and whom they had called Commander of the Faithful. They examined the dates of their respective risings and found that Saʿīd had risen first. Shaybān therefore yielded and marched with him. Before that, Shaybān had encountered a man from Syria named Nuṣayr, who was with a cavalry force. He killed him, and his companions were routed.

The Year 127

The account of the allegiance to Marwān ibn Muḥammad and the deposition of Ibrāhīm ibn al-Walīd

In it, the civil strife broke out.

Ismāʿīl ibn Ibrāhīm said: When al-Walīd ibn Yazīd was killed, Marwān ibn Muḥammad ibn Marwān was governor in Armenia. When news of al-Walīd’s killing reached him, he called the people to pledge allegiance to whomever the Muslims approved, and they pledged allegiance to him. When news of Yazīd ibn al-Walīd’s death reached him, he summoned Qays and Rabīʿa and assigned stipends to twenty-six thousand from Qays and seven thousand from Rabīʿa. He gave them their allotments and appointed Isḥāq ibn Muslim al-ʿUqaylī over Qays and al-Musāwir ibn ʿUqba over Rabīʿa. Then he set out for Syria, appointing his brother ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Muḥammad ibn Marwān as his deputy over al-Jazīra. The leading men of Qays—al-Wathīq ibn al-Hudhayl ibn Zufr, Yazīd ibn ʿUmar ibn Hubayra al-Fazārī, and Abū al-Ward ibn al-Hudhayl ibn Zufr ibn ʿĀṣim ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yazīd al-Hilālī—met him with four or five thousand from Qays. They marched with him until he arrived at Aleppo, where Bishr was…

Masrūr and Bishr, the two sons of al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, were sent by Ibrāhīm ibn al-Walīd when news reached him of Marwān’s march. They drew up against the people, and Abū al-Ward ibn al-Hudhayl ibn Zufar came out with three hundred men. They shouted “Allāhu akbar!” and charged Marwān until they were close to him. Then they turned around, reversed their shields, and joined Marwān. Marwān and those with him charged, and Masrūr and Bishr fled without a fight. Marwān captured them and imprisoned them with him. He also took many of their companions prisoner, but Marwān set them free. Then Marwān marched until he came to Ḥimṣ and called on them to march with him and give allegiance to the two designated successors, al-Ḥakam and ʿUthmān, the sons of al-Walīd ibn Yazīd, who were imprisoned with Ibrāhīm ibn al-Walīd in Damascus. They pledged allegiance to him and went out with him until he reached Sulaymān ibn Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik’s camp, after a fierce battle. Marwān took possession of his camp. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn al-Ḥajjāj ibn ʿAbd al-Malik heard what had befallen Sulaymān; he was encamped in another district, so he set out for Damascus. Ibrāhīm ibn al-Walīd left Damascus and encamped at Bāb al-Jābiya, preparing to fight. He had the funds hastily brought with him and summoned the people, but they deserted him. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn al-Ḥajjāj and Sulaymān ibn Hishām arrived and entered the city of Damascus, intending to kill al-Ḥakam and ʿUthmān, the sons of al-Walīd, who were in prison. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn al-Ḥajjāj and Sulaymān ibn Hishām arrived and entered the city of Damascus, intending to kill al-Ḥakam and ʿUthmān, the sons of al-Walīd, who were in prison. Yazīd ibn Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Qasrī came and entered the prison. He killed Yūsuf ibn ʿUmar and al-Ḥakam and ʿUthmān, the sons of al-Walīd ibn Yazīd, who were “the two lambs.”

Ismāʿīl said: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Wāqid al-Jarmī related to me that Yazīd ibn Khālid killed the two of them. It is said that a client of Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh, called Abū al-Asad, was assigned to kill them; he crushed their heads with clubs. A messenger from Ibrāhīm came to them. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn al-Ḥajjāj went to his house to take his family out, but the people of Damascus rose against him, killed him, and cut off his head. They brought it to Abū Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yazīd ibn Muʿāwiya, who was imprisoned with Yūsuf ibn ʿUmar and his companions. They brought him out and placed him on the pulpit in his fetters, with ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz’s head before him. They removed his fetters while he was on the pulpit, and he addressed them, pledging allegiance to Marwān and reviling Yazīd and Ibrāhīm, the two sons of al-Walīd, and their adherents. He ordered ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz’s body to be crucified upside down at Bāb al-Jābiya, and sent his head to Marwān ibn Muḥammad. When Ibrāhīm heard, he fled and sought a guarantee of safety.

Abū Muḥammad [sought security] for the people of Damascus, so Marwān granted them security and was pleased with them. Then Yazīd ibn Khālid ibn Yazīd ibn Muʿāwiya, Abū Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yazīd ibn Muʿāwiya, Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān, and Abū Bakr ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yazīd came to Marwān. He gave them permission to enter. The first to speak was Abū Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yazīd ibn Muʿāwiya. He greeted him as caliph and offered condolences for al-Walīd and his two sons, al-Ḥakam and ʿUthmān, the sons of al-Walīd. He said, “And the two young men have been struck down. We belong to God. They were indeed the two lambs, eaten and put away.” Then they pledged allegiance to him. He then came to Damascus, ordered Yazīd ibn al-Walīd to be exhumed and crucified, and received the allegiance of the people of Syria.

In this year Ibrāhīm ibn al-Walīd came to Marwān ibn Muḥammad in al-Jazīra, renounced his claim, and pledged allegiance to him. Marwān accepted it from him and granted him security. Ibrāhīm set out and encamped at al-Raqqa, on the bank of the Euphrates. Then a letter came to Marwān from Sulaymān ibn Hishām, asking for a guarantee of safety. Marwān granted him security, and Sulaymān came and pledged allegiance to him. Marwān’s authority was thus established. The reign of Ibrāhīm ibn al-Walīd, who had been deposed, lasted several months.

Abū al-Ḥasan said: Two and a half months.

In this year Yazīd ibn Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Qasrī was killed in al-Ghūṭa. A man from Banū Tamīm called Ṣaʿṣaʿa killed him.

In this year Zāmil ibn ʿAmr killed al-Walīd and Khālid, the two sons of Yazīd ibn al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, at Marwān’s order. In this year Thābit ibn Nuʿaym renounced his allegiance and said, “I am the Qaḥṭānī Yellow One.” In this year the people of Ḥimṣ and Damascus renounced their allegiance to Marwān. Marwān marched until he came to Ḥimṣ and overcame them. He killed some of their leaders, ordered part of their city demolished, and proclaimed a guarantee of safety among the people. Then he sent al-Walīd ibn Muʿāwiya ibn Marwān against Thābit ibn Nuʿaym, who was at Ṭabariyya. He besieged its people, and Thābit was defeated. A great number of his companions were killed. Thābit fled and came secretly to Palestine. Marwān pursued him with ʿAmr ibn al-Waḍḍāḥ and Abū al-Ward. When they learned where he was, they seized him and sent him to Marwān in Damascus. Marwān cut off his hands and feet.

Account of the allegiance to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muʿāwiya in Kūfa

In this year, which was the year 127, the people of Kūfa pledged allegiance to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muʿāwiya ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar Dhū al-Janāḥayn. His two brothers, al-Ḥasan and Yazīd, the sons of Muʿāwiya, were with him.

Ismāʿīl ibn Ibrāhīm related to me. He said: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muʿāwiya ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar and his two brothers, al-Ḥasan and Yazīd, the sons of Muʿāwiya, came to ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz in Kūfa during the governorship of Yazīd ibn al-Walīd. He treated them generously, provided mounts for them, and paid each of them three hundred dirhams every day. When Yazīd died and Ibrāhīm ibn al-Walīd pledged allegiance to Marwān, some of the Shīʿa rose up and called for allegiance to Ibn Muʿāwiya. The one who did this was Hilāl ibn al-Ward, a client of Banū ʿIjl. They brought him in and admitted him to the palace, and the people of Kūfa and Ismāʿīl ibn ʿAbd Allāh, along with those Syrians who were in Kūfa, pledged allegiance to him. He entered and stayed for some days while people pledged allegiance to him. Allegiance to him came from al-Madāʾin and from every direction. On Wednesday he went out, intending to confront Ibn ʿUmar, but there was no fighting between them. Then the people set out for battle the following morning. Mukabbir ibn al-Ḥawārī was killed, along with many Yemenis who were with Ibn Muʿāwiya, and he was defeated and entered the palace. The Zaydiyya remained and fought fiercely, holding the mouths of the lanes until an agreement was made for ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muʿāwiya and his two brothers: they could go wherever they wished in the lands and would not be pursued. Ibn ʿUmar sent word to ʿUmar ibn al-Ghaḍbān ibn al-Qabʿatharī, ordering him to descend to the palace and bring Ibn Muʿāwiya out. ʿUmar ibn al-Ghaḍbān sent to him, and he conveyed him, his Shīʿa who were with him, and those from among the people of al-Madāʾin, the Sawād, and Kūfa who had followed him. ʿUmar’s messengers traveled with them until they took them beyond the bridge. ʿUmar then descended to the palace. After that, Ibn ʿUmar appointed Ismāʿīl ibn ʿAbd Allāh as commander.

The death of Saʿīd ibn Baḥdal and the appointment of al-Ḍaḥḥāk as his successor. In this year, which was the year 127, Saʿīd ibn Baḥdal al-Khārijī died. Ismāʿīl ibn Ibrāhīm related to me that when Saʿīd ibn Baḥdal was at the point of death in Shahrazūr, his commanders gathered around him. He called on them to appoint one of their number as their successor, and entrusted that matter to them. He said to us, “Choose ten from among yourselves.” They selected ten from among them, then reduced them to four. Then he said to the four…

“Choose.” So they chose al-Ḍaḥḥāk ibn Qays al-Muḥallamī and Shaybān ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz al-Yashkurī. Saʿīd said to the two of them, “Choose for the Muslims and for yourselves.” Shaybān said, “I choose al-Ḍaḥḥāk for myself and for the community.” Al-Ḍaḥḥāk said, “I choose Shaybān for myself and for the community.” Shaybān insisted on choosing al-Ḍaḥḥāk, and their companions approved. They pledged allegiance to al-Ḍaḥḥāk. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk recited a verse:

“I shall surely bring men, if I gain power over them, with thrusts that split their heads like the mouths of waterspouts…”

Ismāʿīl ibn Ibrāhīm said: al-Walīd ibn Saʿīd al-Shaybānī related to me that Saʿīd ibn Baḥdal made the succession a council of six, among them al-Ḍaḥḥāk, al-Khaybarī, Shaybān, and ʿUbayda ibn Sawwār al-Taghlibī, who was absent in Azerbaijan. They pledged allegiance to al-Ḍaḥḥāk. Then ʿUbayda arrived and refused to accept al-Ḍaḥḥāk. They said to him, “You must enter into what they have entered, or we will make you feel our spears.” So he pledged allegiance to him. Then al-Ḍaḥḥāk sent Ḥabnāʾ ibn ʿIṣma al-Shaybānī with cavalry to Takrīt, and he gained control of it and sent its revenues to al-Ḍaḥḥāk. He sent Abū al-Rīsh Khālid ibn al-Rīsh to Ḥūlāyā and its surrounding territory. He encountered Jamīʿ ibn Miqran al-Kalbī and Ḥurayth ibn Abī al-Jahm. Jamīʿ was killed, and Ḥurayth fled and went to al-Madāʾin. ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar sent al-Aṣbagh ibn Dhuʾāla, who encamped at al-Madāʾin. Abū al-Rīsh, ʿAbthal, and Ḥabnāʾ ibn ʿIṣma advanced and all met at al-Madāʾin. Al-Aṣbagh ibn Dhuʾāla cut the bridge and withdrew to Kūfa. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk ibn Qays advanced toward Kūfa and encamped at Dayr al-Thaʿālib with three thousand men; the account that gives the larger number says four thousand. ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar sent ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās al-Kindī with ten thousand. They faced one another, with the Euphrates between them. Miskīn said, “ʿUbayd Allāh, choose: if you wish to cross over to us, you have our guarantee that we will not attack you until all those with you have crossed. Or give us the same guarantee, and we will cross over to you.” But he refused that, ʿUbayd Allāh withdrew to Kūfa. Miskīn crossed the Euphrates, and al-Ḍaḥḥāk advanced and encamped on the bank of the Euphrates. The people built crossings, and he crossed. Miskīn marched and found Ibn ʿUmar, the Syrians, and the people of Kūfa positioned at the mouths of the lanes; they had dug a trench. This was on Wednesday, with several nights of Shaʿbān of the year 127 having passed. Miskīn’s companions stormed the trenches, and seventeen people were killed among them, men and women. News of this reached al-Ḍaḥḥāk, who sent Ḥabnāʾ ibn ʿIṣma with some men and charged them not to fight that night. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk advanced with those who were with him and charged at them. When he came within arrow range, he dismounted a band from each squadron; they were eager to fight. The Syrians soon fled, crossed the trenches, and entered Kūfa. Then they turned back immediately. This was on Thursday. They returned to their positions, and some of them attacked the Syrians. ʿĀṣim ibn ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz and Jaʿfar ibn al-ʿAbbās were killed, and the Syrians fled. Then on Friday Ibn ʿUmar went out in the morning, urged the people on, and sent al-Aṣbagh ibn Dhuʾāla with ten thousand. He took the highway as though intending to go to Syria, while al-Ḍaḥḥāk and those with him remained in place, intending to turn toward their camp. They had been informed, however, and had left Shaybān behind in the camp. Al-Aṣbagh and those with him continued until they were opposite al-Ḍaḥḥāk, facing Ibn ʿUmar and his men. Neither side turned toward the other. When night fell, the Syrians left Kūfa, heading in every direction; none of them remained there. In the morning Ibn ʿUmar set out toward Wāsiṭ. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk’s herald proclaimed, “Do not pursue anyone who is turning away, and do not wound anyone. We have given you, people of Syria, three days. Whoever joins what we have joined will have what we have; whoever wishes to go wherever he likes in the land may go in safety. Whoever comes to them, they will attach him to their company; whoever departs, they will not hinder him.” He sent Ḥabnāʾ ibn ʿIṣma to the palace of Kūfa. He sold the spoils and seized many treasuries, weapons, and funds. When the first day of Ramaḍān came, al-Ḍaḥḥāk marched to Wāsiṭ and appointed Malḥān as his deputy in Kūfa. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk marched until he encamped against Ibn ʿUmar at Wāsiṭ and fought him. The champion of the Syrians, and the one directing that battle, was Manṣūr ibn Jumhūr. Jaḥshana, Manṣūr’s nephew, was killed in that battle, and Manṣūr charged ʿIkrima and killed him.

Ismāʿīl ibn Isḥāq said: al-Walīd ibn Saʿīd related to me. He said: One day Manṣūr went out and charged against ʿAbd al-Malik ibn ʿAlqama. He stabbed him, driving the spear through his back, and killed him. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk’s ranks broke up, and they withdrew in grief for him. It is said that the fighting lasted six months; it is also said, a year, until Ibn ʿUmar made peace with him. Ibn ʿUmar sent to al-Ḍaḥḥāk on the condition that he grant him terms of approval and confirm him in his office.

Ismāʿīl said: ʿAwn ibn Yazīd al-Bāhilī related to me. He said: “I was at Wāsiṭ when I saw ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar come to al-Ḍaḥḥāk and grant him terms of approval.” In connection with this, Shubayl ibn ʿAẓra al-Ḍabʿī said:

“Have you not seen that God has made His religion prevail, and Quraysh have prayed behind Bakr ibn Wāʾil…”

ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān led the pilgrimage in the year 127. In the year 127 Abū Isḥāq al-Hamadhānī died—it is said, on the day al-Ḍaḥḥāk ibn Qays entered Kūfa. Abū Ḥuṣayn, Saʿīd ibn Masrūq al-Thawrī, and Jābir ibn Yazīd al-Juʿfī also died. Abū Nuʿaym said: In the year 128. ʿĀṣim ibn Bahdala, a client of Banū Asad, Ismāʿīl ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Suddī, and Jāmiʿ ibn Shaddād—all in Kūfa; Muḥammad ibn Wāsiʿ al-Azdī in Baṣra; Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb in Egypt; and Wahb ibn Kaysān, a client of the Āl al-Zubayr.

The Year 128

Account of the fighting between al-Ḍaḥḥāk and Marwān

In this year al-Ḍaḥḥāk ibn Qays marched until he came to al-Mawṣil. Its governor came out to meet him, and al-Ḍaḥḥāk killed him and took control of the city. When Marwān heard, he wrote to his son ʿAbd Allāh ibn Marwān, who was then in charge of al-Jazīra, ordering him to encamp at Naṣībīn. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk marched against him and besieged him near Naṣībīn for about two months, but gained nothing from him. He sent cavalry on raids throughout the land of al-Jazīra, until his cavalry reached al-Raqqa.

The Syrian lords who had fled from Marwān, Quraysh and others, gathered around al-Ḍaḥḥāk. Marwān set out for Naṣībīn. He departed from ʿAyn al-Warda and encamped at al-Akdar. Then on Monday he left al-Akdar, advancing in battle formation, his infantry marching and his horses unsaddled, while he was in the center. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk met him about two farsakhs from al-Ḍaḥḥāk’s camp, near the time of the noon prayer.

Ismāʿīl said: al-Sarī ibn Muslim and al-Walīd ibn Saʿīd related to me that when the two armies drew close, some of al-Ḍaḥḥāk’s leading Syrian men came to him and said, “By God, no caller summoning people to this cause has ever had such a gathering as you have had since Islam began. Fall back, and send ahead some of your cavalry, infantry, and horsemen to meet this tyrant.” He said, “By God, I have no need of this world of yours. I only sought this tyrant. I have made a vow to God that if I see him, I will charge at him until God judges between me and them. I owe seven dirhams, three of them in my sleeve.” Then Marwān advanced, and they met and fought until sunset. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk was killed in the battle, but no one knew it. Night came between them, and the two sides returned to their camps. About six thousand of them were killed; most of the dead were al-Ḍaḥḥāk’s companions. About eight hundred women from among the Khawārij were killed. When morning came, Marwān ordered a banner of safe conduct to be raised and invited people to come to it. Al-Khaybarī went out and called among his Khawārij: “Whoever wants Paradise and death, let him come forward with me!” Three hundred and fifty horsemen came forward with him. They charged at Marwān in the center, and it gave way, leaving the center exposed. A man from among the Khawārij charged at Marwān and struck him with his sword on the shoulder, severing the baldric and knocking off the cheekpiece of his helmet. Marwān struck him and hit his hand, and he fled.

Ismāʿīl said: al-Sarī, who had witnessed that day, related to me. He said: “That day a fog rose, so that a man could not see the crest of his horse or his whip. Marwān’s troops dispersed in every direction…”

His son ʿAbd Allāh ibn Marwān remained on the right wing, and Isḥāq ibn Muslim on the left, both unaware of Marwān’s situation. Al-Khaybarī came and entered Marwān’s camp. He cut the guy ropes of Marwān’s pavilion and sat on his bed. His companions scattered around the tent, looting and killing, and their battle cry was “O Khaybarī!” The rest of al-Khaybarī’s companions did not know what had happened because of the dust and fog, and they saw al-Khaybarī only after he had been killed. When those in Marwān’s camp saw how few they were, a client of Muḥammad ibn Marwān rose up. There was in his guard a man called Sulaymān ibn Masrūḥ, one of the Berbers. He called out among the slaves, “Whoever follows me is free!” About three or four thousand men, slaves and others, gathered around him. Al-Khaybarī was killed, and the fog cleared from Marwān’s two wings, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Marwān and Isḥāq ibn Muslim.
