They saw the Khawārij’ banners where Marwān had been and said, “Al-Khaybarī has been killed; his companions have carried him off and buried him.” They could not obtain his head or his body. A client of Marwān called Ghazwān rode off at a gallop until he reached Marwān and told him what had happened. Marwān returned to his camp, and the Khawārij held their position. Shaybān then retreated until he encamped at the two Zābs in the land of al-Mawṣil and dug a trench around himself. Marwān came and fought them for ten months; each day Marwān’s banner was beaten. Then Shaybān abandoned the trenches and went to Shahrazūr, then descended to ʿAlā Māh, then to al-Ṣaymara, then came to Kirmān, then came to the island of Barakāwān, and then came to ʿUmān. They fought him there, and he was killed.

Ismāʿīl said: ʿĀṣim ibn al-Ḥadathān related to me. He said: Ḥabīb ibn Jadra al-Hilālī related to me. He said: “I have never seen a woman more grief-stricken than a woman of Banū Shaybān whose father, brother, husband, mother, paternal aunt, and maternal aunt were killed with al-Ḍaḥḥāk. Her eye never ceased weeping, and I never saw her laughing or smiling.” She said:

“Who can reach a heart ravaged by grief, or a soul that has no place of rest…”

… The righteous departed, and the best of them from among a people who departed …

… A people who met their appointed fate; whatever they had sent ahead was good …

… They were steadfast before the swords, neither shrinking from them nor showing cowardice …

… Young men who sold their lives—by the Lord of the House, they did not lose …

… They followed what pleased their Lord when religion and the customary practices had perished …

… The people obtained what they sought; after His favor there are no further favors …

The Revolt of Basṭām ibn Layth al-Thaʿlabī in Ādharbayjān

In this year, the year 128, Basṭām ibn Layth al-Thaʿlabī, one of the Banū Zayd, revolted in Ādharbayjān. He held the view of the Bayhaṣiyya. He killed an agent of Marwān, then came to a town with a little over forty men. Yaḥyā ibn Abī al-Ḥurr marched against them with the people of Mosul and routed them. Then Basṭām came to Qardā. A band of Syrians, more than a thousand strong, passed by him; he attacked them by night and inflicted losses on them. Then he withdrew to Naṣībīn and there killed a man of the Shaybāniyya called Ṭāriq al-Aḥdab. Then he went to Armenia and Ādharbayjān. ʿĀṣim ibn Zayd sent his brother ʿAbd al-Malik against him with six thousand, while he was with about two hundred men. ʿAbd al-Malik and the leading men with him were killed. Then Basṭām marched to Shahrazūr, where Jidār ibn Qays al-Shaybānī, an agent of Marwān, was stationed. Jidār fortified himself against him. Basṭām then set out toward Iraq and encountered ʿAzīz ibn Abī al-Mutawakkil with about two thousand men. He routed them. News of him reached al-Ḍaḥḥāk, who sent Shajarah ibn Zuhayr al-Shaybānī and al-Khaybarī against him. Al-Khaybarī encountered him and attacked him by night, killing Basṭām and most of his companions. In the morning he pursued those of them who remained among the vineyards and gardens, sold the spoils, and withdrew. When Shajarah heard, he withdrew as well.

In the year 128, Bukayr ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Ashajj, Abū ʿImrān al-Juwaynī, and Saʿd ibn Ibrāhīm died.

ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz led the pilgrimage.

The Governorship of Iraq by ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz

Al-Walīd ibn Hishām related to me, from his father, from his grandfather; and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mughīrah, from his father; and Abū al-Yaqẓān and others said: ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz assumed the governorship of Iraq in the year 126. Yazīd ibn al-Walīd appointed him. Al-Ḥajjāj ibn Arṭāh, the jurist, was in charge of his police. Then he proceeded to Wāsiṭ and appointed a man of Kalb over his police. That man remained in charge of his police throughout his governorship. He assigned the policing of Baṣra and Kūfa to their respective governors. His tax clerk was Rūzbihān, and his correspondence clerk was his client al-Ḥakam ibn al-Nuʿmān. Ibn ʿUmar was dismissed when he was not yet forty years old.

The Governorship of Iraq by Yazīd ibn ʿUmar ibn Hubayra

In this year, the year 128, Marwān appointed Yazīd ibn ʿUmar ibn Hubayra governor of Iraq. This was before the killing of al-Ḍaḥḥāk. Yazīd marched until he encamped at Hīt. The news reached al-Muthannā ibn ʿImrān al-ʿĀʾidhī of Quraysh, who was al-Ḍaḥḥāk’s agent over Kūfa. Manṣūr ibn Jumhūr sent against him those of the Khārijites who were with him. Manṣūr encamped at al-Anbār, while Ibn Hubayra marched, leaving the road to al-Anbār until he came out upon ʿAyn al-Tamr. Manṣūr ibn Jumhūr returned, and they met at Ghamr and fought. Manṣūr and his companions were routed until they reached Kūfa. Ibn Hubayra advanced until he came to al-Rawḥāʾ. Al-Muthannā ibn ʿImrān came out against him, but Ibn Hubayra routed them and killed many of them. They left Kūfa that very night. Ibn Hubayra encamped at al-Nukhayla. ʿUbayda ibn Sawwār al-Taghlibī marched against him, with al-Mantūf ibn Sawwār at the head of his vanguard. Ibn Hubayra routed him until he reached the Ṣarāt. He cut the bridge, while ʿUbayda was on the far side of the Ṣarāt. Muṭāʿin ibn Muṭāʿin came from Kaskar with the Khārijites who were with him and encamped at al-Sīb. Ibn Hubayra sent against him a man called ʿAṭiyya al-Taghlibī, who killed Muṭāʿin at al-Sīb. His army remained where it was, with a man called Shaybān over them. ʿUbayda ibn Sawwār advanced and marched against Shaybān’s camp. Ibn Hubayra came up, and they met and fought fiercely. ʿUbayda and those of the Khārijites who stood with him were killed. Manṣūr ibn Jumhūr went on until he reached Ḥulwān and stayed there. He continued moving from one place to another until he reached Sind. Abū Ṭālūt al-Ḥaḍḍī went on until he reached Baṣra and stayed there for some days; then he joined Shaybān ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz al-Yashkurī.

In this year, the year 128, al-Ḥārith ibn Surayj sought refuge with al-Kirmānī and the Azd and said, “Come, let us fight this rebel”—meaning Naṣr ibn Sayyār. They fought Naṣr and routed him. When night fell, Naṣr set out toward Abrshahr. Al-Ḥārith hoped that Tamīm and their Syrians would rally to him. They said, “We are with you.” He turned toward them, and Muḍar rallied to al-Ḥārith and pledged allegiance to him, while Yemen and Rabīʿa rallied to al-Kirmānī. They fought. Al-Ḥārith was killed, but it was not known who killed him. Tamīm was routed, and al-Kirmānī gained control of Marw and wrote the covenants. Concerning this, Naṣr ibn Sayyār said the following about the killing of al-Ḥārith ibn Surayj:

… O you who brought humiliation upon his people … … Away with you, and far removed be you, O ruined one …

… The Azd and their allies … … did not aspire to ʿAmr or Mālik …

ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz led the pilgrimage.

The Year 129

Ismāʿīl ibn Isḥāq said: When Ibn Hubayra killed ʿUbayda ibn Sawwār and his companions, he went to Wāsiṭ. Those who were in the city rose up and blocked the palace gate against Ibn ʿUmar with bricks until Ibn Hubayra came to him. He brought Bishr ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Bishr ibn Marwān before him. He took up his sword and ordered him to enter one of the palace rooms. Ibn Hubayra disliked carrying this out himself and wrote to Marwān about it. Marwān wrote back ordering him to kill Ibn ʿUmar by treachery. Ibn Hubayra disliked that, so Marwān wrote to him to send Ibn ʿUmar to him. He sent him to Marwān, who imprisoned him at Ḥarrān with Ibrāhīm ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAlī.

The Account of Ṭālib al-Ḥaqq’s Revolt in Ḥaḍramawt

In this year, the year 129, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yaḥyā al-Aʿwar al-Kindī, who was called Ṭālib al-Ḥaqq, revolted in Ḥaḍramawt. Ibrāhīm ibn Jablah ibn Makhramah al-Kindī was in charge there. Ṭālib al-Ḥaqq expelled Ibrāhīm from it without fighting. The Ibāḍīs gathered around him and pledged allegiance to him; most of his companions were people of Baṣra. Then he set out for Ṣanʿāʾ, where al-Qāsim ibn ʿUmar al-Thaqafī was in charge, with two thousand men of the Khārijites. Al-Qāsim ibn ʿUmar came out with about thirty thousand men. They met at al-Jāliḥ, a village among the villages of Abyan …

They fought fiercely. Then al-Qāsim was routed, and most of the slain were among his companions, until he reached Ṣanʿāʾ. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yaḥyā marched on, while al-Qāsim had dug trenches. He attacked him by night just before dawn. Al-Qāsim fled, and al-Ṣalt ibn Yūsuf ibn ʿUmar was killed in the battle; many people were killed. ʿAbd Allāh entered Ṣanʿāʾ and seized the treasuries and wealth, thereby strengthening himself. He stayed for some months, then sent to Mecca a man from the people of Baṣra, of the Azd, called Balj ibn al-Muthannā. Then he sent Abū Ḥamza al-Mukhtār ibn ʿAwf al-Azdī with ten thousand men and ordered him to remain in Mecca. Ismāʿīl ibn Isḥāq alleged that Balj arrived during the pilgrimage season. The people were unaware until they were at ʿArafāt and the cavalry appeared to them from the mountain, by way of al-Ṭāʾif. The people gathered around ʿAbd al-Wāḥid ibn Sulaymān ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān, who was governor of Mecca and Medina. ʿAbd al-Wāḥid disliked fighting them, so ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ḥasan ibn Ḥasan ibn ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib went between them until he secured their agreement—and theirs with him—not to do anything until the pilgrimage rites were over. They did so. ʿAbd al-Wāḥid led the people in the standing at ʿArafāt, while Balj stood there with his companions and at Muzdalifa. They stayed through the days of Minā. When the day of departure came, ʿAbd al-Wāḥid departed and went to Mecca. Then Abū Ḥamza came to Mecca and addressed them from the pulpit, saying, “People of Mecca, you taunt me because of my companions, claiming that they are young men.”

The Sermon of Abū Ḥamza

“People of Mecca, you taunt me because of my companions, claiming that they are young men. Were the Companions of the Messenger of God ﷺ anything but young men? Indeed, I know how recklessly you pursue what harms you in your return to God. Were I not occupied with others besides you, I would not have refrained from laying hands upon you. Yes, they are young men made mature while still young: weighty, their eyes unseeing of evil, their feet slow to falsehood. You have seen them …”

… in the depths of the night, their backs bent over the repeated passages of the Qurʾān. When one of them passed a verse mentioning Paradise, he wept out of longing for it; when he passed a verse mentioning the Fire, he gasped, as though the breath of Hell had reached his ears. They joined their night’s exhaustion to their day’s exhaustion. The earth had worn away their foreheads, hands, and knees. Their complexions were sallow, their bodies emaciated from prolonged standing and abundant fasting. They considered all of that little in relation to God. They fulfilled God’s covenant and carried out His promise. When they saw the enemy’s arrows nocked, their spears leveled, and their swords drawn and flashing, and the battle line blazed and thundered with the bolts of death, they considered the battle line’s threat insignificant beside God’s threat. A young man among them would advance until his legs were separated from his horse’s neck; the beauty of his face was stained with blood, his forehead was rubbed in the dust, and the beasts of the earth hastened toward him. How many an eye is in a bird’s beak, though its owner had long wept out of fear of God! How many a hand has been severed at the wrist, though its owner had long leaned upon it in prostration to God in the depths of the night! How many a tender cheek and noble forehead has been cleft by iron clubs! May God have mercy on those bodies and admit their souls to Paradise. Then he said: ‘People are of two kinds: we are of them, except for an idol worshipper, a disbeliever among the People of the Book, an unjust ruler, or one who supports him.’

Ismāʿīl ibn Isḥāq related to us from al-Zanjī ibn Khālid, who said: Abū Ḥamza preached to us in Mecca a sermon that made the clear-sighted doubt and increased the doubter in doubt. He praised and extolled God, then said: ‘People, we asked you about these rulers of yours, and you spoke about them—by God, whom we know—as follows: “They took wealth unlawfully and spent it where it did not belong; they acted unjustly in judgment and appropriated our rights and our spoils, making them a thing passed around among their rich men and those of worldly rank among them. They put our shares and rights toward women’s dowries and the sexual use of slave girls.” We said to you, “Come to these people who wronged us and wronged you, acted unjustly in judgment, and judged by other than what God sent down.” You said, “We do not have the strength for that. We wish we could find someone to suffice us.” We said, “We will suffice you. Then God is our guardian over us: if we prevail, we will give every person entitled to a right his due.” So we came and met …’”

… the spears with our faces and the swords with our chests, and we made God our guardian and guarantor: if we prevailed, we would give every person entitled to a right his due. But you stood between them and us and fought us. May God drive you far away! By God, had you said, “We do not know what you say, nor are we aware of it,” that would have been a more defensible excuse, although there is no excuse for the ignorant. But God willed only that the truth be spoken by your tongues and that He hold you to account for it in the Hereafter.

The Revolt of Shaybān ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz al-Yashkurī

In this year, the year 129, Ibn Hubayra sent Nabāta ibn Ḥanẓala, one of the Banū Abī Bakr ibn Kilāb, to Sulaymān ibn Ḥabīb ibn al-Muhallab. Sulaymān had come to al-Ahwāz when Shaybān ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz withdrew, and he was with him. Sulaymān ibn Ḥabīb sent Dāwūd ibn Ḥātim. They met at al-Mādhiyār, which is Manādhir. Dāwūd and his companions were killed and routed. Dāwūd ibn Ḥātim, Qubayṣa ibn ʿAmr ibn al-Muhallab ibn Qubayṣa ibn al-Muhallab, and Mukhallad ibn Muʿāwiya ibn al-Muhallab were killed.

In this year Ibn Hubayra sent ʿĀmir ibn Ḍibāra of Murra, of Ghatafān, against Shaybān ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz al-Yashkurī, after Shaybān had withdrawn from Marwān. Shaybān sent al-Jawn al-Shaybānī, and they met at al-Sinn. Al-Jawn and his companions were killed. Shaybān went down to Shahrazūr. Marwān wrote to Ibn Ḍibāra, “Do not fight him; whenever he moves from a place, let him settle there.” Shaybān’s companions kept scattering from him until he reached Māh.

The Killing of Shaybān, the Appearance of Abū Muslim al-Khurāsānī, and the Account of Naṣr’s Fighting against al-Kirmānī

Ismāʿīl ibn Isḥāq said: Then Shaybān went to al-Ṣaymara, then to the island of Abr Kāwān, then crossed over to ʿUmān, where he was killed. Ibn Hubayra wrote to ʿĀmir ibn Ḍibāra to proceed against ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muʿāwiya al-Hāshimī. He met him at Iṣṭakhr, with his two brothers, al-Ḥasan and Yazīd, the sons of Muʿāwiya, with him. Ibn Ḍibāra routed him until he reached Khurāsān. Abū Muslim had appeared in Ramaḍān of the year 129, and he imprisoned the Hāshimī and his brothers …

In this year, the year 129, al-Kirmānī marched to Marw al-Rūdh, and Salm ibn Aḥwaz al-Māzinī, Naṣr ibn Sayyār’s governor, marched against him. They met, and al-Kirmānī was routed. Tamīm fell upon the army to plunder, so al-Kirmānī wheeled against them and routed them. This was at night. They returned to their camp and agreed to a truce for three days. At night al-Kirmānī took the route behind the mountain. When morning came, they pursued him and fought. Then they made peace on the condition that al-Kirmānī stay in the village of Bāb ʿAbd al-Qays until they could decide what to do, while Ibn Aḥwaz and his companions stayed in the city of Marw al-Rūdh. Naṣr ibn Sayyār came to them, and they fought for six months, until winter set in and the mounts grew thin. Al-Kirmānī sent to his son, who was in Marw, and his son sent him about a thousand men, as well as clothing and supplies sufficient for them for the year. They were led by ʿAbd al-Jabbār ibn Shuʿayb, a man of the Banū Hanāʾa. A cavalry force of Banū Tamīm met them and routed them, taking everything they had with them. They returned to Marw. The Banū Tamīm who were in Marw rose against al-Kirmānī’s son, led by ʿArfaja ibn al-Ward al-Saʿdī, and besieged al-Kirmānī’s son in the city. News reached them that Naṣr and al-Kirmānī had made peace, so they brought al-Kirmānī’s son out of the city. Naṣr and al-Kirmānī returned to Marw. Al-Kirmānī remained for some days, then withdrew from Naṣr and left that night. When Naṣr had performed the dawn prayer, he went out against them. Envoys moved between them and kept restraining the people from fighting. While matters stood thus, al-Ḥārith ibn Surayj charged with the Banū Ḥanẓala, and fighting broke out. Al-Kirmānī was routed; they caught up with and killed him. A man of the Banū Mujāshiʿ called Muḥārib ibn Hilāl ibn ʿUlaym brought his head. Al-Kirmānī’s son, Rabīʿa, and the Azd reached Sarakhs. They joined Shaybān ibn Maslama, a man of the Banū Sadūs, a Ḥarūrī who had gained control of Sarakhs, Ṭūs, and the region of Abrshahr, with nearly thirty thousand Khārijites. They pledged allegiance to him and joined him. When the Khārijites of Baṣra who were with him saw this, they said, “He has inclined toward worldly life and become partisan.” Mishkān, a client of the Banū Sulaym, went out with five thousand men, and ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Simṭ, a client of Muḍar, left him with about two thousand. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ziyād, a client of Quraysh and a leader among them, stayed at home. Shaybān marched with the remainder of the Ḥarūriyya who were with him in two divisions. Al-Kirmānī’s son sent out five or six thousand men until …

He crossed over to Marw, and Shaybān traveled until he too reached Marw. Naṣr ibn Sayyār traveled with a group of Muḍar toward Ibn al-Kirmānī and met him at a village of Marw called Qār. They fought fiercely; then the Ḥarūriyya were routed, and about three hundred of them were killed. Ibn al-Kirmānī departed that night and camped at a village called Abzan, below his former position. The next morning Naṣr came against him with his companions, fought them, routed them, and killed them in great numbers. Ibn al-Kirmānī escaped. Shaybān entered from the other side, went to the quarter of the Azd, and fortified himself with a trench. Rabīʿa and the Azd gave him their allegiance. Naṣr besieged them and fortified himself with a trench. They fought at those two trenches for about a year and a half.

In this year, which was the year 129, the Ibāḍīs in the Maghrib rose up. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥabīb al-Fihrī seized their leader, Saʿd ibn Masʿūd, killed him, and crucified him. The Ibāḍīs then rose against them under ʿAbd al-Jabbār ibn Maʿn. Yazīd ibn Ṣafwān al-Maʿāfirī met them in Ṣafar of the year 129. The two commanders were killed, and the companions of Ibn Ḥabīb were routed. Abū Qurra al-Ṣifrī also came from Tilimsān in Ṣafar. Ibn Ḥabīb sent Sulaymān ibn ʿUthmān against him. Sulaymān, ʿAbd Allāh, and ʿUthmān were killed, along with notables of the people of Ifrīqiya, and Abū Qurra turned back to Tilimsān.

The pilgrimage was led by ʿAbd al-Wāḥid ibn Sulaymān ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān.

In this year, which was the year 129, Bishr ibn Ḥarb, Abū ʿAmr, and ʿAmr ibn Mālik al-Nakrī died.

Before the year 130, Ghaylān ibn Jarīr, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Isḥāq, the grammarian of Ḥaḍramawt, Maṭar ibn Ṭahmān al-Warrāq, and ʿĀṣim

The Year 130

In it, Naṣr ibn Sayyār and Ibn al-Kirmānī made peace. Al-Kirmānī’s name was ʿAlī ibn Shabīb ibn ʿĀmir ibn Nawārī ibn Ḍayīm ibn Mulayḥ ibn Sharṭān ibn Maʿn ibn Mālik of the Azd. They agreed to fight Abū Muslim and, when they were finished, to consider their affairs. Abū Muslim secretly sent word to ʿAlī ibn al-Kirmānī, “I am with you.” Ibn al-Kirmānī made peace with him and pledged allegiance to him. They all set out against Naṣr. Abū Muslim sent word to Ibn al-Kirmānī, “Engage him in battle.” The two sides fought through the day and night. At dawn Abū Muslim came upon them from behind. When Naṣr saw this, he sent word to Abū Muslim, “I am with you, and I have a greater claim on you than Ibn al-Kirmānī. I will pledge allegiance to you.” Abū Muslim set out with more than ten thousand men and reached the residence. He sent his companions, who struck at the faces of the Azd and Banū Tamīm, and they withdrew. The people made peace. Abū Muslim sent word to Naṣr, “Come.” Naṣr said, “I will perform ablution,” then went out through another gate of his, mounted a barḏawn, and left, leaving Abū Muslim’s messengers sitting there. This was after the afternoon prayer. They sent word to Abū Muslim that Naṣr had fled, and that his companions had fled to the right and left. Abū Muslim traveled that night until he reached Naṣr’s baggage camp at the far end of Marw. He seized Naṣr’s family and young children; his adult sons fled. Naṣr reached Sarakhs and stayed there. Abū Muslim sent Ibrāhīm ibn Bassām, a client of Banū Layth, to Sarakhs. The Ḥarūrī Shaybān and the Rabīʿa who were there fought him. He routed them and killed Shaybān and a great number of Rabīʿa. The people of Ṭūs sent word to Naṣr, “We are with you,” and pledged allegiance to him. Naṣr sent his son Tamīm to reinforce them, with nearly three thousand men. Abū Muslim sent Qaḥṭaba—whose name was Ziyād ibn Shabīb, while Qaḥṭaba was a byname. Qaḥṭaba came to them from upper Ṭūs; troops from the direction of Abū al-Ward came to them; and al-Qāsim ibn Mujāshiʿ arrived with the Black-clad forces from Sarakhs. ʿĀṣim ibn ʿUmayr, with most of the people, went to Qaḥṭaba, who routed him. ʿĀṣim ibn ʿUmayr traveled until he caught up with Naṣr. Naṣr then departed and camped at Qūmis.

ʿAmr ibn ʿUbayda related to me. He said: Qazʿa, the client of Naṣr ibn Sayyār, related to me. He said: Abū Muslim sent word to Naṣr, “Come.” Naṣr went out through another gate of his until he had left the city. He captured Salm ibn Aḥwaz, who was in charge of Naṣr’s police, and killed him.

Muḥammad ibn Muʿāwiya related to me. He said: Bayhas ibn Ḥabīb al-Rām related to me. He said: Abū Muslim appeared in Ramaḍān of the year 129. He imprisoned ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muʿāwiya and his two brothers, then killed him and released his brothers in the year 130. Naṣr ibn Sayyār fled, and Abū Muslim sent Qaḥṭaba ibn Shabīb. Qaḥṭaba met Nabāta, one of the Banū Abī Bakr ibn Kilāb, at Jurjān in Dhū al-Ḥijja of the year 130, and killed Nabāta and his son Ḥabba ibn Nabāta.

Abū al-Dhayyāl said: That day, the people of Khurāsān and the people of Jurjān killed those of Banū Tamīm who were there, as well as the people of the mosques. When Naṣr heard of this, he departed from Qūmis, withdrew to a place off to the side, and wrote to Ibn Hubayra, the governor of Marwān, asking him for reinforcements.

The Battle of Qudayd

In this year, which was the year 130, the Battle of Qudayd took place.

ʿAlī ibn Muḥammad related to me, from Isḥāq ibn Ibrāhīm al-Azdī, who said: When the people departed from Mecca—and that was at the end of the year 129—ʿAbd al-Wāḥid ibn Sulaymān went to Medina and wrote to Marwān informing him that the people of Mecca had failed to support him. Marwān dismissed him and wrote to ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn ʿUmar, his governor in Medina, ordering him to dispatch an army. Abū Ḥamza set out at the beginning of the year 130, intending for Medina. He appointed Abraha ibn al-Ṣabbāḥ al-Ḥimyarī as his deputy in Mecca and put Balj ibn

ʿUqba al-Saʿdī in command of his vanguard. The people of Medina came out, and the two sides met at Qudayd on Thursday, with nine nights of Ṣafar of the year 130 remaining. Balj had thirty thousand horsemen. He said to them, “Clear our way so that we may reach those who have rebelled against us and acted unjustly in their rule. Do not make us take you as our enemies, for we do not want to fight you.” They refused and fought them. The people of Medina were routed, and Abū Ḥamza came to them. ʿAlī ibn al-Ḥuṣayn ibn al-Ḥurr said to him, “Pursue these people and finish off their wounded, for every age has its own rule, and dealing severely with these people is more fitting.” He said, “I do not think so, nor do I think I should depart from the practice of those who came before me.” Abū Ḥamza went on to Medina and entered it on Monday, when thirteen nights of Ṣafar of the year 130 had passed.

Abū al-Ḥasan said, on the authority of a man of the Anṣār, al-Muṣʿab, and others: ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn ʿUmar appointed ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān, son of ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, as commander. The Quraysh banner was with Ibrāhīm ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muṭīʿ. Abū Ḥamza came and camped opposite them. The two sides fought, and both stood firm. Three hundred men of Quraysh were killed. The Āl al-Zubayr fought valiantly that day, and twelve men of them were killed.

Names of those killed at Qudayd

From the Āl of al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām: Ḥamza ibn Muṣʿab ibn al-Zubayr; his son ʿUmāra ibn Ḥamza; Muṣʿab ibn ʿUkāsha ibn Muṣʿab; ʿAtīq ibn ʿĀmir ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr; his son ʿAmr ibn ʿAtīq; Ṣāliḥ ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUrwa ibn al-Zubayr; al-Ḥakam ibn Yaḥyā ibn ʿUrwa ibn al-Zubayr; and al-Mundhir ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Mundhir. Four descendants of Khālid ibn al-Zubayr were killed: Saʿīd ibn Muḥammad ibn Khālid, a son of Mūsā ibn Khālid, one of them called Bahbandhā, and another man. Forty men of Banū Asad were killed. Also killed that day was Umayya ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿUthmān

Ismāʿīl ibn Ibrāhīm related to me. He said: Juwayriya ibn Asmāʾ informed me. He said: ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn ʿAbd Allāh set out intending for Qudayd. His banner fell, and the people took it as an ill omen.

Ismāʿīl said: Ghassān ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥamīd related to me. He said: On the day of Qudayd, Umayya ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿUthmān went out with his face veiled. He looked at no one and spoke to no one, absorbed in his distress, until he was killed.

Abū al-Ḥasan said: The people heard no mourners whose lament was more heart-rending than the mourners of Qudayd. Not a household remained in Medina without someone in it weeping. A female mourner said, lamenting them:

“What have time and I to do with one another? ... Qudayd has wiped out its men ...”

“... I shall weep in private, and I shall weep in public ...”

Ismāʿīl ibn Isḥāq related to us. He said: Marwān ibn Muḥammad ibn Marwān sent Muḥammad ibn ʿAṭiyya al-Saʿdī of Saʿd ibn Bakr with four thousand of his troops, most of them garrison troops. They stipulated to Marwān, “When we have killed al-Aʿwar, we will return; you will have no authority over us.” He granted them that. Ibn ʿAṭiyya came and met Balj at Wādī al-Qurā, as Balj had set out intending for Syria. They fought; Balj and most of his companions were killed. Ibn ʿAṭiyya kept killing them until they entered Medina. About a thousand men among them rallied around a man of theirs called al-Ṣabbāḥ, from Hamdān. He took refuge on one of the mountains of al- and fought them there for three days. Then

When ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yaḥyā al-Aʿwar heard, he set out with about thirty thousand men. Ibn ʿAṭiyya camped at Tabāla, and al-Aʿwar camped at Ṣaʿda. Then they met, and al-Aʿwar was routed. He went to Jarash, while Ibn ʿAṭiyya set out, and the two met and fought until night came between them. At dawn Ibn ʿAṭiyya was still in his position. Al-Aʿwar came down with about a thousand men from the people of Ḥaḍramawt and fought until he and those with him were killed. He sent al-Aʿwar’s head to Marwān.

Ibn ʿAṭiyya traveled until he reached Ṣanʿāʾ. A man of Ḥimyar called Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmayr ibn al-Sabbāq revolted against him and seized the garrison. Ibn ʿAṭiyya sent his nephew ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Yazīd against him. Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh was routed, and some of his companions were killed. Yaḥyā went on until he reached ʿAdan Abyan and gathered about two thousand men. Ibn ʿAṭiyya set out against him in person and met him in a valley of theirs. Yaḥyā and those with him were killed, and Ibn ʿAṭiyya returned to Ṣanʿāʾ.

Then a man called Yaḥyā ibn Ḥarb of Ḥimyar emerged on the seacoast. Ibn ʿAṭiyya sent against him a man of Kinda, known by the kunya Abū Umayya, who was in charge of al-Waḍḍāḥiyya. Yaḥyā and some of his companions were killed. Then Ibn ʿAṭiyya set out against ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿīd, al-Aʿwar’s deputy, who was with a group of Ḥaḍramawt in a large force. Ibn ʿAṭiyya attacked them at dawn and fought them until night sheltered him. Then Marwān’s letter came ordering him to lead the prayer at the pilgrimage season. He invited the people of Ḥaḍramawt to make peace, and they made peace with him. Ibn ʿAṭiyya set out in haste with fifteen men from among his leading companions, leaving his nephew ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Yazīd behind. Ibn ʿAṭiyya hurried on and camped in a valley of Murād.

At a village called Shibām they fell upon him, killed him and his companions, and cut off his head. Some men of Hamdān came and buried his body in a village called Khaywān, on the route taken by the pilgrims of Yemen. When ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Yazīd heard, he sent a man of al-Waḍḍāḥiyya called Shuʿayb al-Bāriqī with cavalry and ordered him to kill everyone he found. Shuʿayb killed the men, cut open the women, killed the children, seized the property, uprooted the palm trees, and burned the villages. Then he returned to ʿAbd al-Raḥmān.

The pilgrimage was led by Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAṭiyya al-Saʿdī.

In it, al-Ṣaqr ibn Ayyūb al-Fazārī came with a large force to Tilimsān in Ramaḍān of the year 130. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥabīb came out against him. Al-Ṣaqr ibn Ayyūb was killed, and the Berbers were routed. Sulaymān ibn Dharrāq al-Murhabī, who was a Ṣifrī, came forward. Ibn Ḥabīb went out against him, but Sulaymān then turned back without fighting.

In this year, which was the year 130, Muḥammad ibn al-Munkadir died in Medina, as did ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Bakr ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAmr ibn Ḥazm in Medina, Yazīd ibn Rūmān, Abū al-Zinād, and Ismāʿīl ibn Abī Ḥakīm, all of them in Medina. Mālik ibn Dīnār, Shuʿayb ibn al-Ḥabḥāb, Yazīd al-Rishk, Abū al-Tayyāḥ, Kulthūm ibn Jabr, Ḥabīb al-Muʿallim, and Yaḥyā al-Bakkāʾ all died in Baṣra. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Ṣuhayb, ʿĀmir al-Aḥwal, ʿAlī ibn al-Ḥakam al-Banānī, Ḥumayd ibn Qays al-Aʿraj, and Yazīd ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Qusayṭ [died] in Medina; and Abū Wajza al-Saʿdī

The Year 131

In it, Qaḥṭaba ibn Shabīb set out from Jurjān after the killing of Nabāta. When Ibn Hubayra heard, he sent ʿĀmir ibn Ḍubāra down to Iṣṭakhr and dispatched his son Dāwūd ibn Yazīd ibn ʿUmar ibn Hubayra. Dāwūd and ʿĀmir traveled from Iṣṭakhr to Iṣfahān. Ibn Hubayra sent Mālik ibn Adham al-Bāhilī with a great cavalry force, and al-Muṣʿab ibn Ṣaḥṣaḥ al-Asadī and Ghuṭayf al-Sulamī, acting in concert. Some of them camped at Māh and some at Hamadhān. Qaḥṭaba sent his son al-Ḥasan against those armies. When the armies learned of al-Ḥasan’s advance, they joined together at Nihāwand, and al-Ḥasan arrived and besieged them.

Muḥammad ibn Muʿāwiya related to me, from Bayhas, Abū Ḥabīb ibn Ḥabīb, who said: Qaḥṭaba set out and met ʿĀmir ibn Ḍubāra and Dāwūd. The two sides met at Jābala, a district of Iṣfahān, in Rajab, on Saturday, with seven days of Rajab of the year 131 remaining. ʿĀmir was killed, and Dāwūd was routed and caught up with his father. Qaḥṭaba caught up with those who were with him until he besieged the people of Nihāwand together with his son al-Ḥasan ibn Qaḥṭaba.

In this year, which was the year 131, ʿĀmir was killed. Naṣr ibn Sayyār wrote to Marwān and Ibn Hubayra asking them for reinforcements, but no reinforcements reached him before Qaḥṭaba advanced. Naṣr withdrew and camped at Rayy. He fell ill, then set out and died at Hamadhān.

ʿAmr ibn ʿUbayda related to me. He said: Qazʿa, the client of Naṣr ibn Sayyār, related to me. He said: Naṣr died at Sāwa, in the land of Rayy. We buried him and let water flow over his grave.

Qazʿa said: When death came upon him, he summoned his sons and said, “Beware of the cities; go to Syria. If the Marwānids’ term lasts, you will be with them; if matters turn out otherwise, what befell them will befall you.” He recited to me these verses by Naṣr ibn Sayyār, when reinforcements were slow to arrive:

“I see amid the ashes a flicker of an ember, One destined to become a blaze ...”

“For fire is kindled by two firesticks, and action is preceded by speech ...

... I say in wonder: would that I knew ... ... Are the Umayyads awake or asleep ...”

Bihays ibn Ḥabīb said: Ibn Hubayra wrote to Marwān, informing him of Ibn Ḍubāra’s killing. He sent al-Ḥawthara ibn Suhayl al-Bāhilī, of Banū Firāṣ, to him with ten thousand men, exclusively from Qays. The armies gathered at Nihāwand, and Ibn Hubayra appointed Mālik ibn Adham over them.

Bihays said: Qaḥṭaba besieged the people of Nihāwand for nearly four months.

ʿAmr ibn ʿUbayda related from Qazʿa, who said: “We remained there until we ate our riding animals, and severe hunger and hardship befell us.”

Bihays said: Then Mālik ibn Adham made a truce with Qaḥṭaba, and the city was opened in Shawwāl of the year 131. Qaḥṭaba killed the people of Khurāsān who had fled with Naṣr ibn Sayyār, saying, “I did not make peace on behalf of the people of Khurāsān; I made peace only on behalf of the people of Syria.” Mālik claimed that he had made peace on behalf of the people of Khurāsān and the people of Syria.

Abū al-Dhayyāl said: The people of Syria were granted security except for two men: a Qurashī called Ibn al-Bukhturī, and a man of Banū Sulaym called Sufyān. He had them both killed in cold blood.

Qazʿa said: Qaḥṭaba stationed men at the city gates and let no prominent person among the people of Khurāsān escape killing. He seized the sons of Naṣr ibn Sayyār and killed them.

Abū al-Ḥasan said: He took it by treaty at the beginning of Dhū al-Qaʿda. Saʿīd ibn al-Ḥurr ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, who was from the people of al-Jazīra, was killed, as was Ḥātim ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Surayj.

Bihays ibn Ḥabīb said: When Qaḥṭaba had finished with Nihāwand, he set out intending to go against Ibn Hubayra. Ibn Hubayra advanced with ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās al-Laythī at his vanguard, until he encamped at Rāz al-Rūz, between Ḥulwān and al-Madāʾin.

Bihays said: Al-Ḥawthara ibn Suhayl reached us at a river called Tāmrā. Those of the companions of ʿĀmir ibn Ḍubāra who were there joined us, as did those who had come out of Nihāwand. The two forces came together in a total of fifty-three thousand men drawing stipends. Al-Ḥasan ibn Qaḥṭaba marched at his father’s vanguard and encamped at Ḥulwān. His father came to him, and all the troops assembled. Ibn Hubayra marched and encamped at Jalūlāʾ al-Waqīʿa, while Qaḥṭaba encamped at Khāniqīn, four parasangs between the two camps. This was at the end of Dhū al-Qaʿda in the year 131. Our vanguards and theirs kept meeting for days; neither we nor they withdrew.

In that year, the plague was in Baṣra.

ʿAlī ibn Muḥammad related to me: The plague began in Jumādā II. People were dying in groups during it, and likewise in Rajab. It grew severe in Shaʿbān, and its fever and severity were at their height in Ramaḍān and Shawwāl. Then it abated, returning to something like how it had begun, until the year ended. During the plague, Ayyūb al-Sakhtiyānī and ʿAlī ibn Zayd ibn Judʿān died.

In this year, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Najīḥ al-Makkī and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn al-Qāsim ibn Abī Bakr died.

Al-Ḥajj was led by al-Walīd ibn ʿUrwa ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAṭiyya, of Saʿd ibn Bakr.

After the year 130, ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Ḥakīm al-Ḥaḍramī and ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Rafīʿ died.

Al-Rukayn ibn al-Rabīʿ, Abū al-Aswad Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, and ʿUmāra ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ḍubāra. During Marwān’s caliphate, Abū al-Ḥuwayrith died; his name was ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Muʿāwiya al-Murādī, a client of Banū Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf.

The Year 132

The account of the defeat of Ibn Hubayra at Wāsiṭ

In it, Qaḥṭaba ibn Shabīb met Yazīd ibn ʿUmar ibn Hubayra.

Muḥammad ibn Muʿāwiya related to me from Bihays ibn Ḥabīb, who said: Ibn Hubayra heard that Qaḥṭaba had set out in the direction of al-Mawṣil. Ibn Hubayra said to his companions, “Why are the people avoiding us?” They said, “They intend for Kūfa.” Ibn Hubayra called for departure, and did not stop until he reached Rāz al-Rūz, six parasangs from our trench. We left our fodder and provisions behind. Qaḥṭaba came and encamped at a trench, while we encamped and found ourselves in the open country. He stayed for about twenty days, until his animals were fattened and rested. Then he marched, moving crosswise toward the north wind, until he crossed the Tigris at Bāḥamshā. This was in summer, when the unripe dates had reddened and the water had diminished. He forded the river, and came on; we too came on, all intending for Kūfa, until we all reached the Euphrates. He encamped in the open country, and we encamped on the Euphrates embankment in the land of Upper al-Fallūja. This was on Tuesday, with eight days of Muḥarram having passed, in the year 132. Then Qaḥṭaba crossed the Euphrates, and about seven hundred crossed with him. About the same number joined us. Ibn Hubayra came without being aware of him. They took position on the embankment, while we were below them. We charged them, and they pushed us from our position by about two hundred cubits. Then we returned against them and routed them until they reached the embankment. Qaḥṭaba was struck by a thrust in the face, fell into the Euphrates, and perished, while we did not know it, nor did they know.

Abū al-Dhayyāl said: Qaḥṭaba was killed, and Ibn Hubayra’s companions fled until they reached the mouth of the Nile. Bihays said: We kept pushing them back, and they kept pushing us back, until the moon set. That was with eight days of Muḥarram having passed. Then we went on, not knowing where to turn, until we caught up with the people at Sūrāʾ. We crossed the ford of Sūrāʾ, and many people drowned; much baggage was lost. After we had crossed, the people gathered. A herald called, “Whoever wants Syria, come here!” A group of people went with him, though we did not know them. Another called, “Whoever wants al-Jazīra,” and another called, “Whoever wants Kūfa.” With each one went a group of people. I said, “Whoever wants Wāsiṭ, come here!” We reached the Qanāṭir al-Sīb by morning. Ibn Hubayra came, and we all encamped at the mouth of the Nile. Al-Ḥawthara ibn Suhayl came; he had not entered Kūfa before he met us at the mouth of the Nile. Then we set out until we entered Wāsiṭ on Friday, the Day of ʿĀshūrāʾ. The blacks awoke and found their commander missing, so they searched for him and brought him out. He had a thrust wound in his forehead. They buried him on Wednesday, appointed al-Ḥasan ibn Qaḥṭaba over themselves, and set out for Kūfa. Ziyād fled, as did a secretary of Ibn Hubayra called ʿĀṣim ibn Abī ʿĀṣim, one of the clients of Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥarb. Abū Salama sent al-Ḥasan ibn Qaḥṭaba, accompanied by Khāzim ibn Khuzayma, to Wāsiṭ.

Bihays said: Al-Ḥasan ibn Qaḥṭaba came to us at the end of Muḥarram in the year 132 and encamped at al-Māḥūz. Then he came to us in Ṣafar, not intending to fight, but only wanting to reconnoiter a place to stay. He brought laborers to dig a trench. The people said to Ibn Hubayra, “Let us fight the people!” but he refused. They persisted until he said, “O Muslim, open the gates,” and appointed his son Dāwūd, Muḥammad ibn Nubāta, and Maʿn ibn Zāʾida in the center.

Bihays said: When Abū Jaʿfar came to us, they advanced against us in full force, and we began fighting them, until news of Marwān’s defeat reached us. We remained in the fighting through Shaʿbān, Ramaḍān, and Shawwāl. Al-Ḥasan ibn Qaḥṭaba came to us at the end of Shawwāl and said, “To whom are you stretching out your necks? No one remains who has not entered into obedience to the Commander of the Faithful. You have God’s pledge and covenant that you are secure in everything that came before.” The next morning Khāzim ibn Khuzayma came to us and said the same. Then al-Ḥārith ibn Nawfal al-Hāshimī came to us, then Isḥāq ibn Muslim al-ʿUqaylī came to us and said, “The people will give you whatever you want.” We drew up between us a written agreement of peace. This was at the beginning of Dhū al-Qaʿda in the year 132, on terms of our choosing: that Ibn Hubayra would remain in charge of his affairs, with five hundred of his companions, and stay for fifty days in the eastern city, without giving allegiance. When that period was over, he could go to a place of safety if he wished, or join what the people had joined. Whatever was in our hands would remain ours. We opened the gates on Saturday, after several days of Dhū al-Qaʿda had passed. They entered the city, went around in it, and left. They did the same on Sunday. On Monday, one of their non-Arab men entered on horseback and searched out every animal bearing God’s brand, seized them, and said, “These belong to the command.”

Bihays said: I informed Abū ʿUthmān, who informed Ibn Hubayra. He said, “By the Lord of the Kaʿba, the people have betrayed us!” He said to Abū ʿUthmān, “Go to Abū Jaʿfar, greet him on my behalf, and tell him: ‘If you think fit, grant us permission to come to you.’” He granted permission. Ibn Hubayra rode out on Monday, and about two hundred of us rode with him, until we reached the portico.

Ibn Hubayra, Abū ʿUthmān, Saʿīd, and I dismounted. We went on foot with him until we reached the door of the chamber. The door was raised, and there was Abū Jaʿfar seated. Ibn Hubayra said to him, “Peace be upon you, O Commander, and God’s mercy and blessings.” “Lower the door.” I heard Abū Jaʿfar say, “O Yazīd, we, the Banū Hāshim, overlook the wrongdoer and take the better course. To us, you are unlike others: you have been loyal, and the Commander of the Faithful is most desirous of showing favor to someone like you. So expect what will please you.”

Abū al-Ḥasan said: Ibn Hubayra said to him, “Your rule is new. Let the people taste its sweetness and spare them its bitterness, and you will win their hearts. I have continued to await this summons.” Then he stood up. Abū Jaʿfar said, “How astonishing—a man who tells me to kill this one!”

Bihays said: On Monday, with thirteen nights remaining in Dhū al-Qaʿda of the year 132, Abū Jaʿfar sent Khāzim ibn Khuzayma, who killed Ibn Hubayra. The one who carried out the killing was ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Bukhturī al-Khuzāʿī. They also killed Riyāḥ ibn Abī ʿUmāra, a client of the Umayyads, and ʿUbayda Allāh ibn al-Ḥabḥāb, the secretary. They killed Dāwūd ibn Yazīd ibn ʿUmar ibn Hubayra. Khāzim ibn Khuzayma brought out ʿUthmān, Ibn Hubayra’s secretary, and killed him. He seized Bishr ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Bishr ibn Marwān, Abān ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, al-Ḥawthara ibn Suhayl, and Muḥammad ibn Nubāta. Al-Ḥasan ibn Qaḥṭaba sat in the mosque of Ḥassān al-Nabaṭī, on the Tigris toward al-Madāʾin. They brought them to him, and he had their heads struck off. Al-Ḥārith ibn Quṭn al-Hilālī was brought to him, and he ordered him sent to prison. They sought Khālid ibn Salama al-Makhzūmī but could not find him. Their herald announced that Khālid ibn Salama was safe. He came out a day after the people had been killed, and they killed him too.

Sufyān ibn Muʿāwiya calls for the oath of allegiance to Banū Hāshim. In this year, the year 132, Sufyān ibn Muʿāwiya ibn Yazīd ibn ...

Abū ʿUbayda, Abū al-Yaqẓān, and others said: Abū Sufyān ibn al-ʿAlāʾ, Salama ibn ʿAlqama al-Māzinī, ʿAbbād ibn Manṣūr, ʿĀmir ibn ʿUbayda al-Bāhilī, ʿUthmān al-Battī, Ismāʿīl al-Makkī, and Muʿāwiya ibn ʿUmar al-Ghallābī acted as intermediaries between the two. He accepted the truce and they made peace, and they wrote an agreement between them that Salm would stay in the governor’s residence and Sufyān among the Azd until they saw what Ibn Hubayra would do. This reached Abū Salama al-Khallāl, who wrote to Balj ibn al-Muthannā ibn Makhrama al-ʿAbdī: “If Sufyān fights Salm, then [all is well]; otherwise, you are the commander.” Sufyān resolved to fight and marched against Salm, sending his son Muʿāwiya ahead. Muʿāwiya was killed, and Sufyān was routed.

In this year, Rūḥ ibn Ḥātim killed Isḥāq al-Ḍabbī, who had been seeking reward [for his service]. Salm ibn Qutayba sent Ibn Raʾlān against him and routed him. Then Rūḥ withdrew that night, went to Maysān, and took control of it.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAlī’s dispatch to fight Marwān

In this year, the year 132, Abū al-ʿAbbās sent his uncle ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās to fight Marwān ibn Muḥammad. Marwān advanced with those from the people of Syria and al-Jazīra who were with him. The Banū Umayya mustered in person, along with their followers. Bishr ibn Yasār related to me from a shaykh, one of the people of al-Jazīra, who said: Marwān set out with a hundred thousand cavalrymen from Syria and al-Jazīra.

Abū al-Dhayyāl said: Marwān had one hundred and fifty thousand men. He marched until he encamped at the two Zābs, below al-Mawṣil. ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAlī marched, and they met on Saturday morning, with eleven days of Jumādā II having passed, in the year 132. Marwān was defeated, cut the bridge, and went to al-Jazīra, where he seized the treasuries and hoards. Then he went to Damascus. ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAlī marched until he entered al-Jazīra, then left and appointed Mūsā ibn Kaʿb al-Tamīmī as his deputy. ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAlī set out for Syria, and Abū al-ʿAbbās sent Ṣāliḥ ibn ʿAlī until the two of them met. Then he marched to Damascus. They besieged its people for several days, then took it by storm. They killed al-Walīd ibn Muʿāwiya, and ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAlī seized when he entered Damascus: Yazīd ibn Muʿāwiya ibn Marwān and ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār ibn Yazīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān. He sent them to Abū al-ʿAbbās, who had them crucified. ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAlī entered Damascus in Ramaḍān of the year 132. Marwān was then in Palestine. He fled until he reached Egypt, and ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAlī killed more than eighty men of the Banū Umayya.

Abū al-Dhayyāl said: Marwān was in Egypt. When news reached him that ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAlī had entered Damascus, he crossed the Nile and cut the bridge, then marched toward al-Ḥabasha. ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAlī sent his brother Ṣāliḥ ibn ʿAlī in pursuit of Marwān. Ṣāliḥ arrived after Marwān had crossed, so Ṣāliḥ appointed ʿĀmir ibn Ismāʿīl, one of Banū al-Ḥārith ibn Kaʿb, and dispatched him against Marwān. He caught up with him in a village in Egypt called Būṣīr. Marwān was killed in Dhū al-Ḥijja of the year 132. Bakr ibn ʿAṭiyya related to me from his father, who said: “I was in Kūfa when Marwān’s head was brought and set up on a spear at the mosque gate. The hopes of whoever among the partisans of Banū Umayya remained were cut off.”

Al-Walīd ibn Hishām related to us from his father, from his grandfather; and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mughīra related from his father; and Abū al-Yaqẓān and others said: Marwān was born in al-Jazīra in the year 72. His mother was a slave woman who had belonged to Muṣʿab ibn al-Zubayr. He was killed at Būṣīr at the end of Dhū al-Ḥijja in the year 132. Ḥātim ibn Muslim said: He was killed in the year 132. His rule lasted five years, ten months, and ten days, until he was killed. The pilgrimage was led by Dāwūd ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās.

In this year, the year 132, Manṣūr ibn al-Muʿtamir, Isḥāq ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Ṭalḥa, Ṣafwān ibn Sulaym, and Muḥammad ibn Abī Bakr ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAmr ibn Ḥazm all died in Medina.

In that year, ʿUmāra ibn Abī Ḥafṣa and Yaḥyā ibn Abī Isḥāq al-Ḥaḍramī died in Baṣra, and Abū Sinān Ḍirār ibn Murra al-Shaybānī died in Kūfa. Also in that year, Abū al-Muʿallā al-ʿAṭṭār died in Baṣra, and Sālim al-Afṭas was killed in al-Jazīra.

During Marwān’s governorship, Khubayb ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, Abū Jaʿfar Yazīd ibn al-Qaʿqāʿ al-Qāriʾ, the client of Ibn ʿAyyāsh, Shayba ibn Naṣṣāḥ, Yaḥyā ibn Khallād al-Zuraqī, Abū al-Ḥuwayrith al-Zuraqī, Saʿīd ibn Sulaymān ibn Zayd ibn Thābit, and Ismāʿīl ibn Muḥammad ibn Saʿd ibn Mālik died.

Names of the officials of Marwān ibn Muḥammad

Baṣra was in a state of turmoil until Ibn Hubayra arrived in the year 129. He wrote to al-Miswar ibn ʿAbbād ibn Ḥuṣayn, ordering him to lead the people in prayer. He went down to the governor’s residence, but Banū Saʿd prevented him. The people reached an agreement on ʿAbbād ibn Manṣūr, who was a judge, and he led the people in prayer. It is also said that Ibn Hubayra asked ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī ʿUthmān, and he led the people in prayer until Salm ibn Qutayba ibn Salm ibn ʿAmr al-Bāhilī arrived as governor of Baṣra on behalf of Ibn Hubayra. Sufyān ibn Muʿāwiya assumed leadership and fought Salm, but Salm defeated him. Then Salm left Baṣra when Ibn Hubayra surrendered, appointing Muḥammad ibn Jaʿfar al-Hāshimī of Banū Nawfal as his deputy in Baṣra. Then Abū al-ʿAbbās sent Asad ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mālik al-Khuzāʿī, and he led the people in Friday prayer. Then Abū Salama appointed Sufyān ibn Muʿāwiya.

Kūfa: al-Ḍaḥḥāk ibn Qays Milḥān al-Shaybānī was appointed over it, but he was killed. Saʿd the Eunuch was appointed, and he was called the Eunuch only because he had no beard; he was a man of al-Azd. Then he was dismissed, and al-Muthannā ibn ʿImrān al-ʿĀʾidhī of Quraysh was appointed, until Ibn ʿUmar made peace with al-Ḍaḥḥāk. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk then returned to

Marwān appointed Ibn ʿUmar over Kūfa: ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥamīd. Then he dismissed him and appointed Ismāʿīl ibn ʿAbd Allāh. Then he dismissed him and appointed ʿAbd al-Ṣamad ibn Abān ibn al-Nuʿmān ibn Bashīr al-Anṣārī. Then Ibn Hubayra arrived and appointed Ziyād ibn Ṣāliḥ al-Ḥārithī over Kūfa. He remained in office until Qaḥṭaba approached, whereupon he appointed Muḥammad ibn Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Qasrī as chief, removed Ziyād ibn Ṣāliḥ, and called people to the cause of Banū Hāshim. Ibn Hubayra sent Ḥawthara ibn Suhayl al-Bāhilī, but he did not reach Kūfa and returned to Ibn Hubayra. Al-Ḥasan ibn Qaḥṭaba entered Kūfa and handed it over to Abū Salama al-Khallāl. Abū Salama confirmed Muḥammad ibn Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh in office. Then Abū al-ʿAbbās ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAlī appeared.

Khurāsān: Naṣr ibn Sayyār remained in office there until Abū Muslim expelled him from it.

Sijistān: Bujayr ibn al-Sahlab gained control of it until Ibn Hubayra arrived and appointed ʿĀmir ibn Ḍubāra al-Murrī. Abū Muslim dispatched Mālik ibn al-Haytham, one of the people of Khurāsān. Then he sent ʿUmar ibn al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿUmayr ibn ʿUṭārid ibn Ḥājib ibn Zurāra. Then he sent Ismāʿīl ibn ʿImrān.

Al-Sind: Manṣūr ibn Jumhūr gained control of it. He killed Marwān while Marwān was there.

Al-Baḥrayn: al-Walīd was killed while Bishr ibn Salām al-ʿAbdī was in charge there. Bishr remained in office until Ibn Hubayra arrived and confirmed him in it. When Bishr ibn Salām died, Sayyār ibn Bishr was appointed. When he died, his brother Salm ibn Bishr was appointed, and he remained in office until Marwān was killed.

Al-Yamāma: al-Bahī, a man of Banū Ḥanīfa, gained control of it. When he died, ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Nuʿmān al-Ḥanafī was appointed, and he remained in office until Abū al-ʿAbbās was acclaimed.

Medina: Marwān confirmed ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān over it. Then, in the year 129, he dismissed him and appointed ʿAbd al-Wāḥid ibn Sulaymān ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān. Then he withdrew from Abū Ḥamza, who had taken Medina. Marwān sent ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAṭiyya of Saʿd ibn Bakr, who killed Abū Ḥamza and added Mecca to his jurisdiction. Then ʿAbd al-Malik went to Yemen and appointed al-Walīd ibn ʿUrwa ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAṭiyya as his deputy. Then Marwān ibn Muḥammad appointed Yūsuf ibn ʿUrwa ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAṭiyya over it, and he remained in office until the acclamation of Abū al-ʿAbbās.

Mecca: ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn ʿUmar was confirmed over it along with his governorship of Medina. Then ʿAbd al-Wāḥid ibn Sulaymān ibn ʿAbd al-Malik was appointed over it. He withdrew from Abū Ḥamza al-Khārijī, and Abū Ḥamza al-Khārijī stayed there. Then Abū Ḥamza left, intending to go to Medina, and appointed Abrāha ibn al-Ṣabāḥ as his deputy. Then Abū Ḥamza returned, and ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAṭiyya killed him. Then ʿAbd al-Malik went to al-Ṭāʾif and appointed Rūmī ibn Māʿiz al-Kilābī. Then he dismissed him and appointed Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Malik. Then ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Muḥammad was killed in a region of Yemen, by treachery. Marwān appointed Yūsuf ibn ʿUrwa ibn Muḥammad, and he remained governor until the acclamation of Abū al-ʿAbbās.

Ifrīqiya: ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥabīb al-Fihrī held control of it until he was killed in the year 138.

Yemen: When the turmoil occurred, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yaḥyā rose up and drove al-Ḍaḥḥāk ibn Zaml out of it. Marwān sent ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Muḥammad, who killed ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yaḥyā. Then he went down, intending to go to Mecca, but was treacherously killed in one of the regions. Marwān appointed Yūsuf ibn ʿUrwa over it along with his governorship of Mecca and Medina. He sent his brother al-Walīd ibn ʿUrwa to Yemen, and he remained governor until the acclamation of Abū al-ʿAbbās.

Armenia: When al-Walīd was killed, Marwān withdrew from Armenia and appointed ʿĀṣim ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yazīd al-Hilālī as his deputy. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk ibn Qays sent Musāfir ibn al-Qaṣṣāb, who killed ʿĀṣim ibn ʿAbd Allāh. The news reached Marwān, and he appointed ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muslim. When he died, Isḥāq ibn Muslim was appointed. Isḥāq left, and the people of Bardhaʿa chose Musāfir ibn Bujayr, until Abū al-ʿAbbās rose to power.

Judicial appointments

Judgeship of Baṣra: During the days of Ibn Suhayl, ʿAbbād ibn Manṣūr served as judge. Then Salm ibn Qutayba arrived, dismissed ʿAbbād, and appointed Muʿāwiya ibn ʿUmar al-Ghullābī. He served as judge for three days, then asked to be excused, and Salm excused him.

He appointed ʿĀmir ibn ʿUbayda al-Bāhilī, but he asked to be excused. So ʿAbbād ibn Manṣūr was appointed, and he remained judge until Abū al-ʿAbbās was acclaimed.

Kūfa: Ibn Abī Laylā was in charge there until al-Ḍaḥḥāk ibn Qays entered it and appointed Ghaylān ibn Jāmiʿ al-Muḥāribī over it. Then Ibn Hubayra arrived and appointed al-Ḥajjāj ibn ʿĀṣim al-Muḥāribī as judge. Then he dismissed him and appointed Manṣūr, who remained until Abū al-ʿAbbās was acclaimed.

Medina: ʿUthmān ibn ʿUmar al-Taymī of Quraysh was in charge there, and ʿAbd al-Wāḥid ibn Sulaymān confirmed him. Then Muḥammad ibn ʿImrān al-Taymī served as judge for al-Walīd ibn ʿUrwa ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAṭiyya at the end of Marwān’s rule.

The pilgrimage season: In the years 127 and 128, ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān; in the year 129, ʿAbd al-Wāḥid ibn Sulaymān ibn ʿAbd al-Malik; in the year 130, ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAṭiyya; and in the year 131, al-Walīd ibn ʿUrwa ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAṭiyya.

Marwān’s chief of police: Kawthar ibn al-Aswad al-Ghanawī.

The army and tax registers, treasuries, and storehouses: ʿImrān ibn Ṣāliḥ, the client of Hudhayl.

Secretary of correspondence: ʿAbd al-Ḥamīd the Elder.

The small seal: ʿAbd al-Aʿlā ibn Maymūn ibn Mihrān.

The caliphate seal: a client of his.

His chamberlain: Saqlāb; it is also said, Miqlāṣ, a client of his.

Al-Walīd ibn Hishām related to me, from his father, from his grandfather; and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mughīra, from his father; and Abū al-Yaqẓān and others said: Marwān was born in al-Jazīra in the year 72. His mother was a slave woman belonging to Muṣʿab ibn al-Zubayr. He was killed in a village of Egypt called Būṣīr, on Thursday, with six nights remaining in Dhū al-Ḥijja, in the year 132.

Marwān’s rule lasted five years, ten months, and ten days.

Yazīd ibn ʿUmar ibn Hubayra took over Iraq. He arrived in the year 129 and appointed Ziyād ibn Suwayd al-Murrī as his chief of police in Wāsiṭ. He was killed on Laylat al-Furāt. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Bashīr al-ʿIjlī was in charge of the police in Kūfa.

Baṣra, concerning its officials: Ibn Rālan was in charge of the police for Salm ibn Qutayba in Baṣra. Ibn Hubayra’s tax secretary was ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-Ḥabḥāb, the client of Banū Salūl; he was killed when Abū Hubayra was killed.

The caliph’s correspondence: Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr, the nephew of Rabāḥ ibn Abī ʿUmāra.

Ibn Hubayra was killed in Wāsiṭ on Monday, with thirteen nights remaining in Dhū al-Qaʿda, in the year 132. He was about forty years old.

Chapter 16

The caliphate of al-Saffāḥ

In the year 132, Abū al-ʿAbbās ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib was acclaimed. His mother was Rayṭa, daughter of ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Madān al-Ḥārithī. He was acclaimed in Kūfa on the night of Friday, with thirteen nights of Rabīʿ al-Awwal elapsed in the year 132, among Banū Awd, at the residence of al-Walīd ibn Saʿd, the client of Banū Hāshim. When morning came, he rode out and led the people in prayer on Friday. On that same day, the general acclamation took place.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mughīra related to me, from his father, who said: I saw Abū al-ʿAbbās when he went out to the Friday prayer, riding a grayish-white barḏawn close to the ground, between his uncle Dāwūd ibn ʿAlī and his brother Abū Jaʿfar. He was a handsome young man, with a yellowish pallor. He came to the mosque and ascended the pulpit and spoke. Then Dāwūd ibn ʿAlī ascended and stood two steps up the pulpit. He praised God and extolled Him, then said: “People, by God, no caliph after ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib has ever ascended this pulpit of yours except this nephew of mine.” He made promises to the people and gave them hopes.

My father said: Then I saw him at the second Friday prayer, and it was as though his face were a shield and his neck a silver ewer; the yellow pallor had gone. By God, there was only a week between the two occasions. In that year ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās killed al-Ghamr ibn Yazīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān, Isḥāq ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Ṭalḥa, and ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Malik. ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAlī killed ʿUmar ibn Abī Salama ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf. In that year Dāwūd ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās killed ʿImrān ibn Mūsā ibn ʿAmr ibn Saʿīd, Yaḥyā and Ismāʿīl, the sons of Umayya ibn ʿAmr ibn Saʿīd, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAnbasa ibn Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ and his two sons Muḥammad and ʿIyāḍ, the sons of ʿAbd Allāh, and Ayyūb ibn Mūsā ibn ʿAmr ibn Saʿīd. Dāwūd ibn ʿAlī performed the pilgrimage.

The Year 133

In it Dāwūd ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās died on the first day of Rabīʿ al-Awwal. In this year the Byzantine tyrant Constantine ibn Alyūn advanced and encamped against Malatya. They fought him fiercely, and he pressed them until they accepted terms of safe-conduct. He demolished the city, the congregational mosque, and the governor’s residence, and sent cavalry along with the Muslims, which escorted them to safety.

In that year Abū al-ʿAbbās sent Muḥammad ibn al-Naḍr ibn Yarīm al-Ḥimyarī, who reached Ṭuwāla in Byzantine territory.

Ziyād ibn ʿUbayd Allāh al-Ḥārithī performed the pilgrimage.

In this year, the year 133, ʿAṭāʾ ibn Abī Muslim al-Khurāsānī, the client of Hudhayl, died. His mother

The Year 134

In it Abū al-ʿAbbās moved away from Kūfa and settled in al-Anbār. In that year, while ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAlī was in Syria, al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Ḥarashī was sent. The Byzantines, with Kūshān the patrician, marched against them. This was in Jumādā al-Ākhira of the year 134. Muqātil ibn Ḥakīm al-ʿAtakī sent his son Mukhallad ibn Muqātil, who met the Byzantines in the Fourth Armenia. Mukhallad was routed and abandoned his army.

ʿĪsā ibn Mūsā ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās performed the pilgrimage. In that year Yaḥyā ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās inspected the people of Mosul and killed them. In that year al-Mughīra ibn Miqsam al-Ḍabbī, Ismāʿīl ibn Muḥammad ibn Saʿd ibn Mālik, and Yazīd ibn Yazīd ibn Jābir al-Azdī died in Syria. In that year Mūsā and al-ʿAbbās, the sons of al-Walīd ibn Yazīd, arrived in the Maghrib, as did ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Ṭalḥa and Saʿīd ibn ʿAmr ibn Sulaym al-Zuraqī.

The Year 135

In it Abū al-ʿAbbās wrote to ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās, ordering him to dispatch military expeditions. He appointed Saʿd ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Raḥbī, but he did not cross the frontier passes, and he stationed [them] at Dābiq and the entrances to the passes. Sulaymān ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās performed the pilgrimage. In that year Yaḥyā ibn Yaḥyā al-Ghassānī, Dāwūd ibn al-Ḥuṣayn, the client of the family of ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Bakr ibn Ḥazm, Abū Ḥāzim, and Mūsā ibn ʿUqba died. During the caliphate of Abū al-ʿAbbās, Ibn Khuthaym and ʿUmar ibn ʿĀmir died.

The Year 136

In it Abū al-ʿAbbās ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās died. Al-Walīd ibn Hishām related to me, from his father, from his grandfather; and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mughīra, from his father; and Abū al-Yaqẓān and others said: Abū al-ʿAbbās was born at al-Ḥumayma in Syria in the year 108. He died in al-Anbār on Sunday, with thirteen days of Dhū al-Ḥijja elapsed. He was twenty-eight years old. ʿĪsā ibn ʿAlī performed the funeral prayer over him. His rule lasted four years and nine months. Then Abū Jaʿfar ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās was acclaimed; his mother was an umm walad.

Names of the officials of Abū al-ʿAbbās

Baṣra: He dismissed Sufyān ibn Muʿāwiya from it and appointed ʿUmar ibn Ḥafṣ Hazar Mard. Then, in the year 133, he dismissed him and appointed Sulaymān ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās, who remained in office until Abū al-ʿAbbās died.

Kūfa: Abū al-ʿAbbās appointed his uncle Dāwūd ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās over it. Then he dismissed him and sent him to lead the pilgrimage season. He appointed ʿĪsā ibn Mūsā ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās over Kūfa, and he remained there until Abū al-ʿAbbās died.

Mecca: Dāwūd ibn ʿAlī governed it along with Medina. Dāwūd died and appointed his son Mūsā ibn Dāwūd as his deputy. Abū al-ʿAbbās dismissed him and appointed his maternal uncle Ziyād ibn ʿUbayd Allāh al-Ḥārithī over it along with Medina and al-Ṭāʾif. Ziyād ibn ʿUbayd Allāh appointed his nephew ʿAlī ibn al-Rabīʿ over it, and he remained there until Abū al-ʿAbbās died.

Medina: ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās sent Ḥusayn ibn Jaʿfar ibn Tammām ibn al-ʿAbbās from Damascus when he entered it. Its governor, Yūsuf ibn ʿUrwa ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAṭiyya, left it. Then Abū al-ʿAbbās appointed his uncle Dāwūd ibn ʿAlī over it, together with Mecca and Ṭāʾif. He governed it for four months, then died and appointed his son Mūsā ibn Dāwūd as his successor. Abū al-ʿAbbās removed him and appointed his maternal uncle Ziyād ibn ʿUbayd Allāh. Ziyād arrived in Medina in Jumādā I, in the year 133, and remained in charge of it until Abū al-ʿAbbās died.

Yemen: Abū al-ʿAbbās combined it with Dāwūd ibn ʿAlī’s governorship and appointed Dāwūd over it. Then ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥamīd al-Khaṭṭābī governed it. Abū al-ʿAbbās then appointed his maternal cousin Muḥammad ibn Yazīd ibn ʿUbayd Allāh over it. When he died, Abū al-ʿAbbās combined it with the governorship of his maternal uncle Ziyād ibn ʿUbayd Allāh. Ziyād appointed his nephew ʿAlī ibn al-Rabīʿ over it, then ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Rabīʿ, who remained in office until Abū al-ʿAbbās died. During Abū al-ʿAbbās’s caliphate, ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥamīd al-Khaṭṭābī had also governed it.

Khurāsān: Abū Muslim remained there.

Sijistān: Abū Muslim sent Mālik ibn al-Haytham, one of the people of Khurāsān, who drove the Syrians out of it. Then he sent ʿUmar ibn al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿUmayr ibn ʿUṭārid ibn Ḥājib ibn Zurāra. Then he sent Ismāʿīl ibn ʿAmr. It is said that he sent Muḥammad ibn al-Ashʿath before ʿUmar ibn al-ʿAbbās.

Sind: Abū al-ʿAbbās sent a man from Banū Tamīm called Mughlis. Manṣūr ibn Jumhūr took him prisoner and killed most of his companions. Abū al-ʿAbbās then dispatched Mūsā ibn Kaʿb al-Marrī. Manṣūr met him at Qandābīl, and Mūsā killed Manṣūr and entered al-Manṣūra. He remained there until Abū al-ʿAbbās died.

Baḥrayn: al-Masīḥ ibn al-Ḥawwārī ibn Ziyād ibn ʿAmr al-ʿAtakī, then ʿAbd Allāh ibn Sulaymān ibn al-Mundhir ibn al-Jārūd, then ʿUmar ibn Ḥafṣ Hazārmard.

Yamāma: Dāwūd ibn ʿAlī. When he died, al-Sirrī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib was appointed.

Jazīra: When Marwān was defeated at the Zāb, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAlī marched and entered it. He then appointed Mūsā ibn Kaʿb al-Marrī as his successor. Abū al-ʿAbbās then appointed his brother, Abū Jaʿfar, over the Jazīra, Armenia, and Azerbaijan. He then ordered him to go to Mecca to lead the pilgrimage. He went and appointed Muqātil ibn Ḥakīm al-ʿAtakī as his successor, who remained in office until Abū al-ʿAbbās died.

The summer expedition: al-Naḍr ibn Yarīm, then Ṣāliḥ ibn ʿAlī.

The pilgrimage season:

Year 132: Dāwūd ibn ʿAlī.

Year 133: Ziyād ibn ʿUbayd Allāh al-Ḥārithī.

Year 134: ʿĪsā ibn Mūsā.

Year 135: Sulaymān ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās.

Year 136: Abū Jaʿfar ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAlī, Commander of the Faithful.

The judiciary

Judgeship of Baṣra: During Abū al-ʿAbbās’s caliphate, Abū Jaʿfar sent the jurist al-Ḥajjāj ibn Arṭāh al-Nakhaʿī as judge over Baṣra. He served as judge for some months. Sulaymān arrived at the beginning of the year 133, removed al-Ḥajjāj, and reinstated ʿAbbād ibn Manṣūr. Then he removed him and appointed Sawwār ibn ʿAbd Allāh and ʿUmar ibn ʿĀmir al-Sulamī, and the two used to sit together. Sawwār asked to be excused, and he excused him, appointing ʿUmar ibn ʿĀmir. ʿUmar died, and he appointed Ṭalḥa ibn Iyās al-ʿAdawī, of ʿAdī al-Ribāb. Then he removed him and reinstated ʿAbbād ibn Manṣūr, who remained in office until Abū al-ʿAbbās died.

Kūfa: Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Abī Laylā, until Abū al-ʿAbbās died.

Medina: Ziyād ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Abī Sabra was appointed in the year 136.

Abū al-ʿAbbās’s security force: ʿAbd al-Jabbār ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Azdī.

His seal and guard: Asad ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mālik al-Khuzāʿī.

His chamberlain: Abū ʿUmāra, his freedman.

In this year, the year 136, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās renounced allegiance and called people to support his own claim.

In it Abū Muslim performed the pilgrimage. Abū al-ʿAbbās had written to ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAlī, ordering him to raid the lands of the Romans and campaign there. ʿAbd Allāh came to Dābiq and encamped there. The troops gathered to him, and news reached him of Abū al-ʿAbbās’s death.

In it Rabīʿa ibn Abī ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, the freedman of the family of al-Madhkūr; ʿAṭāʾ ibn al-Sāʾib; Yazīd ibn Abī Ziyād; ʿUrwa ibn Ruwaym; al-ʿAlāʾ ibn al-Ḥārith, one of the people of Syria; and ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmayr al-Lakhmī, one of the people of Kūfa and an ally of Banū ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb, died.

In it Mūsā ibn Kaʿb killed Manṣūr ibn Jumhūr at Qandābīl on the two nights remaining in the month of Ramaḍān.

In the year 136, ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Muʿāwiya ibn Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik arrived in the land of the Maghrib.

Chapter 17

The caliphate of Abū Jaʿfar al-Manṣūr

The Year 137

In the year 137, Abū Muslim met ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAlī, and they fought fiercely. Then ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAlī was defeated and went to Baṣra. Abū Jaʿfar sent word to Abū Muslim: “Keep what is in your hands …”

… and Abū Muslim became angry and set out for Khurāsān. Abū Jaʿfar sent Salama ibn Saʿīd ibn Jābir, who was Abū Muslim’s relative by marriage—his maternal aunt was married to Abū Muslima. Salama caught up with Abū Muslim before he entered al-Rayy and asked him to come to Abū Jaʿfar. He came with him, while Abū Jaʿfar was at al-Madāʾin. Abū Jaʿfar killed him at al-Rūmiyya. That was on Wednesday, with four nights remaining in Shaʿbān, in the year 137.

I heard Yaḥyā ibn al-Musayyib say: “He killed him while he was in the pavilions.” Then he sent word to ʿĪsā ibn Mūsā, informing him of that and giving him the head and the money. He went out with them and scattered the money, and the people were occupied with it. It is said that Abū Jaʿfar sent Jarīr ibn Yazīd ibn Jarīr ibn ʿAbd Allāh to Abū Muslim.

In it Sinbādh incited the people of al-Rayy, and they rebelled.

Abū ʿUbayda said: Abū Jaʿfar sent Muḥammad ibn al-Ashʿath against them; he killed them and took their children captive. It is also said: Jahūr ibn Marrār al-ʿIjlī.

In it Abū Jaʿfar removed Sulaymān ibn ʿAlī from Baṣra and appointed Sufyān ibn Muʿāwiya over it. Sufyān arrived in the month of Ramaḍān and killed Ibn al-Muqaffaʿ.

In it ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās was brought.

Ismāʿīl ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās led the pilgrimage. There was no summer expedition that year.

In it Ḥuṣayn ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, ʿĀṣim ibn Kulayb, and Asad ibn Wadāʿa died in Syria.

At the beginning of Abū Jaʿfar’s caliphate, al-ʿAlāʾ ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, the freedman of al-Ḥurqa, and Sulaymān ibn Suḥaym died. 4. The year 138.

In it Mulabbad ibn Ḥarmala, one of Banū Abī Rabīʿa, revolted in al-Mawṣil. Abū Jaʿfar dispatched Khāzim ibn Khuzayma against him, and he killed him. It is also said that Mulabbad revolted in the year 137.

In it Ṣāliḥ ibn ʿAlī raided and encamped at Dābiq. Constantine ibn Alyūn, the tyrant of the Romans, came with one hundred thousand men. Ṣāliḥ met him, killed and captured people, and came away safely.

Al-Faḍl ibn Ṣāliḥ ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās led the pilgrimage.

In it Abū Jaʿfar removed Sufyān ibn Muʿāwiya from Baṣra and appointed ʿUmar ibn Ḥafṣ Hazārmard over it.

In it ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥabīb al-Fihrī was killed in Ifrīqiya. He was killed by Ilyās ibn Ḥabīb and his brother ʿAbd al-Wārith.

The Year 139

Jaʿfar ibn Ḥanẓala al-Bahrānī set out and came to Malatya, which was in ruins. He encamped, and ʿAbd al-Wāḥid set out and came to Malatya. He planted crops and prepared a great deal of lime, then withdrew. The tyrant then sent someone, who burned the crops.

Al-ʿAbbās ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās led the pilgrimage.

In it Yūnus ibn ʿUbayd, Dāwūd ibn Abī Hind—who kept people from performing the pilgrimage—Yazīd ibn Usāma ibn al-Hād, ʿAbd Rabbih ibn Saʿīd, the brother of Yaḥyā ibn Saʿīd, and ʿAmr ibn al-Muhājir died in Syria.

The Year 140

In it the Commander of the Faithful, Abū Jaʿfar, wrote to Ṣāliḥ ibn ʿAlī, ordering him to build the city of al-Maṣṣīṣa. He dispatched Jibrīl ibn Yaḥyā, who remained on the frontier there until he built it and completed it in the year 141.

In it Abū Jaʿfar sent ʿAbd al-Wahhāb ibn Ibrāhīm ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAlī to build Malatya. He remained there for a year, until he had built it and settled people in it.

Abū Jaʿfar, Commander of the Faithful, led the pilgrimage.

In it Muṭarrif ibn Ṭarīf, the freedman of Banū al-Ḥārith ibn Kaʿb, and Abū Isḥāq al-Shaybānī, Abū al-ʿAlāʾ Ayyūb ibn Miskīn, and ʿUmāra ibn Ghaziyya.

Before the year 140, Salama ibn ʿAlqama al-Māzinī, ʿUthmān ibn Ḥakīm, Yaʿqūb ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, the freedman of al-Ḥurqa, and ʿUthmān ibn ʿUrwa ibn al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām died. After the year 140, Sharīk ibn Abī Namir, Mūsā and Ibrāhīm, the two sons of ʿUqba, and Saʿd ibn Isḥāq died.

The Year 141

In it the slaves revolted in Baṣra. Sawwār ibn ʿAbd Allāh held the judgeship, the prayer leadership, and the office of assistance. Ḥafṣ ibn al-Naḍr al-Sulamī, who was Sawwār’s chief of police, went out against them and killed the ʿUbayd.

In it Abū Jaʿfar appointed Muḥammad ibn Abī ʿUyayna over the sea. He landed at the island-city of Qays in the sea. The vessels of al-Mīdh came to him, but he did not go out against them. His son went out and was killed along with a group of Muslims. Ibn Abī ʿUyayna abandoned the city, and the enemy then … it; it has been in ruins up to the present day.

In it Sulaymān ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās, Abān ibn Taghlib, and Saʿd ibn Saʿīd ibn Qays, the brother of Yaḥyā ibn Saʿīd, died.

Ṣāliḥ ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās led the pilgrimage.

The Year 142

In it Abū ʿAwn, the governor of Egypt, sent al-ʿAwwām ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz al-Bajalī with one thousand horsemen. Abū al-Khaṭṭāb al-Ibāḍī, whose name was ʿAbd al-Malik ibn al-Samḥ, the freedman of Maʿāfir, Mālik ibn Sumayrān, sent a force against him. They met at Tripoli. Al-ʿAwwām was defeated, and most of his companions were killed.

In it al-Manṣūr, the Commander of the Faithful, Abū Jaʿfar, arrived in Baṣra, and his army encamped at the Greater Bridge.

Ismāʿīl ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās led the pilgrimage.

In it Khālid ibn Mihrān al-Ḥadhdhāʾ died in Baṣra, as did al-Ḥasan ibn ʿAmr al-Fuqaymī, al-Ḥasan ibn ʿUbayd Allāh, and Ḥabīb ibn Abī ʿUmra—all of these in Kūfa.

In it Abū Jaʿfar appointed Maʿn ibn Zāʾida over Yemen, and appointed ʿUmar ibn Ḥafṣ to Baṣra for his second term. Then he appointed him over Sind.

The Year 143

In it Ḥumayd ibn Mihrān al-Ṭawīl and Sulaymān ibn Ṭarkhān al-Taymī, both in Baṣra, died; as did Layth ibn Abī Sulaym, Ashʿath ibn Sawwār, and Mujālid ibn Saʿīd, all in Kūfa; Yaḥyā ibn Saʿīd al-Anṣārī and Muḥammad ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAlqama, among the people of Medina; and Saʿīd ibn Iyās al-Jarīrī and Ḥajjāj al-Ṣawwāf, in Baṣra.

ʿĪsā ibn Mūsā ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās led the pilgrimage.

In it Muḥammad ibn al-Ashʿath, governor of Egypt, sent Abū al-Aḥwaṣ al-ʿAbdī with six thousand men to Ifrīqiya. He encamped at Barqa and met Abū al-Khaṭṭāb al-Ibāḍī near Barqa. Abū al-Aḥwaṣ was defeated and returned to Barqa, while Abū al-Khaṭṭāb went on to Tripoli. Muḥammad ibn al-Ashʿath met him at Labda. Abū al-Khaṭṭāb was killed, and Ibn al-Ashʿath entered al-Qayrawān.

In it Abū Jaʿfar appointed ʿĪsā ibn ʿAmr al-Saksakī, whose teknonym was Abū al-Jamal; he removed him and appointed Ismāʿīl ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās. Abū Jaʿfar removed al-Haytham ibn Muʿāwiya from Mecca and appointed al-Sirrī ibn ʿAbd Allāh. He removed Muḥammad ibn Khālid from Medina and appointed Riyāḥ ibn ʿUthmān al-Murrī.

The Year 144

In it, Ṣāliḥ ibn ʿAlī dispatched Muslima ibn Yaḥyā, the brother of Jibrīl ibn Yaḥyā, and he built the fortress of Adana.

Abū Jaʿfar, Commander of the Faithful, led the pilgrimage.

In it, Sufyān ibn Muʿāwiya ibn Yazīd ibn al-Muhallab was appointed over Baṣra.

In it, Riyāḥ ibn ʿUthmān al-Murrī was appointed over Medina.

In it, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Shibrima al-Ḍabbī, Mūsā al-Juhanī, ʿAmr ibn ʿUbayd at Marrān, Muḥammad and Anas, the two sons of Abī Yaḥyā, and Isḥāq ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Farwa died.

In it, Abū Jaʿfar, Commander of the Faithful, sent down ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Ḥasan ibn al-Ḥasan ibn ʿAlī, Abū Muḥammad, and Ibrāhīm.

Before the year 145 AH, al-Ḥajjāj ibn Arṭāh and Abū Ḥayyān al-Taymī died.

The Year 145

In it, Ṣāliḥ ibn ʿAlī appointed ʿĪsā ibn Kathīr al-Naqqāsh. He raided Selucia, then went to Ṭuwāna, then went to Qarma, where he killed and took captives.

The revolt of Muḥammad al-Nafs al-Zakiyya and his brother Ibrāhīm.

In it, Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Ḥasan ibn al-Ḥasan ibn ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib revolted in Medina in Rajab. Riyāḥ ibn ʿUthmān al-Murrī mobilized, and Ibrāhīm ibn ʿAbd Allāh revolted in Baṣra on the first night of Ramaḍān. Abū Jaʿfar sent ʿĪsā ibn Mūsā, with Ḥumayd ibn Qaḥṭaba at the head of his vanguard. They met, and Kathīr ibn al-Ḥuṣayn, one of the Banū ʿAbd al-Dār, was appointed over Medina. He appointed ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn al-Muṭṭalib; then Abū Jaʿfar dismissed him and appointed ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Rabīʿ al-Ḥārithī, who appointed Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz al-Zuhrī as judge.

Muslima ibn Thābit said: “Ibrāhīm ibn ʿAbd Allāh revolted on the first night of Ramaḍān, and we went out with him. He came to the cemetery of Banū Yashkur, and a group gathered around him; I was among them. Then, when morning came, he marched to the governor’s residence. Sufyān ibn Muʿāwiya ibn Yazīd ibn al-Muhallab was there as governor, and he surrendered the residence without a fight.

“I heard Yasār ibn ʿUbayd Allāh say: ‘I was present that day. Jaʿfar and Muḥammad, the two sons of Sulaymān ibn ʿAlī, came with their clients and those who had joined them—about three thousand men. Ibrāhīm sent al-Ṭahawī against them. He encountered them in Sikkat al-Mirbad, beside the Mosque of the Ḥarūriyya. Jaʿfar and Muḥammad soon gave way.’

“I heard Abū Marwān say: ‘I was present with them that day, and Jaʿfar and Muḥammad’s men were pelting them with arrows.’

“He said: ‘I looked and saw al-Ṭahawī place his forehead on the pommel of his saddle. Safīh drew his sword and charged the people; he struck the hand of the man carrying their standard and severed it. The standard fell, and they fled.’

“Ibrāhīm led the people in the Festival of Breaking the Fast prayer. News of his brother’s death came to him while he was on the pulpit. Then Ibrāhīm left Baṣra and appointed his son al-Ḥasan ibn Ibrāhīm as his deputy. He went as far as Bājimīrā in the rural district of Kūfa, where ʿĪsā ibn Mūsā met him. Ibrāhīm was killed in Dhū al-Qaʿda of the year 145 AH. Bishr al-Raḥḥāl and a large group were killed with him. Ibrāhīm had sent al-Mughīra ibn al-Fazʿ al-Tamīmī, one of the Banū Kaʿb ibn Saʿd ibn Tamīm, to al-Ahwāz, and he took it after fierce fighting. He also sent [men] to Wāsiṭ, and they took it. Salama ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥamīd, client of Banū Rāsib, and Sulaymān ibn Mujāhid, client of Banū Ḍubayʿa, disputed [authority]. Sulaymān ibn Mujāhid prevailed and led the people in the Friday prayer, but no notable attended it.”

Khalīfa related to us: He said: My father related to me that his father had informed him that he attended the Friday prayer but did not pray in the mosque—“Tamām Waṣf.” Then Jaʿfar ibn Sulaymān ibn ʿAlī arrived and led the people in prayer on the Day of Sacrifice. Al-Sarī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib led the people in the pilgrimage.

In it, Ḥabīb ibn al-Shahīd died in Baṣra; Ismāʿīl ibn Abī Khālid and ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Abī Sulaymān al-ʿArzamī died in Kūfa; ʿAmr ibn Maymūn, one of the people of al-Jazīra, died; ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿUmar, one of the people of Medina, died; Yaḥyā ibn al-Ḥārith al-Dhimārī, one of the people of Syria, died; and Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Ḥasan ibn al-Ḥasan ibn ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib died.

The Year 146

ʿAbd al-Wahhāb ibn Ibrāhīm ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās led the pilgrimage.

In it, ʿAwf ibn Abī Jamīla al-Aʿrābī, Ashʿath ibn ʿAbd al-Malik al-Ḥamrānī, Hishām ibn ʿUrwa ibn al-Zubayr in Baghdad, al-Ajlaḥ al-Kindī, and Muḥammad ibn al-Sāʾib al-Kalbī died.

In it, Abū Jaʿfar dismissed ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Rabīʿ al-Ḥārithī from Medina and appointed Jaʿfar ibn Sulaymān ibn ʿAlī, who retained Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz as judge.

In it, Abū Jaʿfar appointed Salm ibn Qutayba over Baṣra for a short time, then dismissed him and appointed Muḥammad ibn Sulaymān, then dismissed him and appointed Muḥammad ibn Abī al-ʿAbbās. The people of Baṣra nicknamed him Abū al-Dibs. ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd Allāh, client of Ghufra bt. Rabāḥ, the sister of Bilāl ibn Rabāḥ, died after the defeat.

In it, ʿAlī ibn Mūsā was dismissed from Kūfa, and Muḥammad ibn Sulaymān ibn ʿAlī was appointed over it.

The Year 147

In it, Abū Jaʿfar pledged allegiance to his son al-Mahdī and removed ʿĪsā ibn Mūsā, who had been the heir apparent.

In this year, the stars fell.

Abū Jaʿfar, Commander of the Faithful, led the pilgrimage.

In it, Hishām ibn Ḥassān al-Qurdūsī died in Baṣra; ʿUthmān ibn al-Aswad died in Mecca; and Yazīd ibn Abī ʿUbayd and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿīd ibn Abī Hind died. It is also said [that they died] in the year 148 AH. Before the year 148 AH, Ḥusayn al-Muʿallim, ʿUthmān al-Battī, and Ḥabīb ibn Shihāb al-ʿAnbarī died.

The Year 148
